The brief also explicitly calls out: *"Start with the problem you're solving, then pick the tools that actually help you solve it."* Our angle — ThorAI reading/writing its own encrypted memory, human controls the key — is exactly the kind of demo-as-the-product story they're looking for.

[16:44 UTC] TRAV:
And the website we build will it be on ipfs or should we host on railway? What are your thoughts.

[16:44 UTC] THOR AI:
Strong case for **IPFS frontend + Railway backend**. Here's the thinking:

**Why IPFS for the frontend:**
- We're building an encrypted memory system on Filecoin — hosting our own demo on IPFS is dogfooding the stack. Judges will notice that immediately.
- The hackathon explicitly values "more on-chain artifacts." IPFS deployment is part of that story.
- We already know how to do it (thorai.eth workflow)

**Why Railway for the backend:**
- Lit Protocol key operations and Filecoin writes need server-side logic — you can't expose private keys in a browser
- The agent memory read/write API needs to run somewhere persistent
- Railway is fast to spin up and we've done it before

**So: static demo frontend on IPFS, agent memory API on Railway.**

The pitch writes itself — *"ThorAI's memory lives on Filecoin, the demo lives on IPFS, the keys are controlled by Lit Protocol smart contracts. Nothing on a centralized server except the glue."* That's clean and on-theme.

One question we need to answer before we start building: **do we want the frontend to be a live interactive demo** (watch the agent write/read memory in real-time) or more of a technical explainer with a manual trigger? That determines how complex the UI needs to be.

**On the conversationLog** — agreed on both counts. Don't chase confirming it as a requirement, just build it anyway. But the key insight is *start capturing it now*. Filecoin programmatic writes I can figure out (similar to Arweave/Turbo). But Lit's threshold key management + access conditions is non-trivial. We should spike that *first*, before touching the frontend or the website. If Lit is harder than expected, we need to know on day 2, not day 7.

**One pushback on Opus** — "full raw logs in the repo." Worth thinking about what's in those. Our architecture decisions are fine, but if any session context bleeds in that has sensitive config or keys, that's a problem. I'll be careful about what I commit to the public repo.

What is Lit Protocol

Copy page

Decentralized key management and private compute

The Naga Mainnet is now live! Check out the blog post here.
You can stake your tokens to support the network and earn rewards via the Staking Portal.
​
Overview
Lit Protocol is a decentralized key management and private compute network. Lit provides developers building in Web3 with three core primitives:
Decentralized Signing and Wallet Management (support for ECDSA and EdDSA signatures) via MPC TSS + TEEs
Decentralized Encryption / Decryption (identity-based BLS encryption scheme) via MPC TSS + TEEs
Private Compute executed via Lit’s MPC TSS network and protected by sealed TEEs
​
The Problem
Lit Protocol is a decentralized key management network that solves the fundamental Web3 security dilemma: how to manage secrets without compromising security, user experience, or decentralization. The network empowers developers to create secure, decentralized applications that manage crypto assets, private data, and user authority seamlessly across any blockchain or distributed system.
Traditional approaches force an impossible choice. Giving private keys or credentials to centralized servers creates massive security responsibilities and single points of failure. Putting the burden entirely on end users requires them to navigate complex key management and opens up avenues for compromise. Lit Protocol eliminates this tradeoff.
Lit’s distributed architecture ensures secrets remain verifiably secure - encrypted at the hardware level and fragmented across a decentralized network with no single point of failure. Using threshold signature schemes (MPC TSS) as a foundation means that for the first time, universal accounts can be programmed and automated without trusting centralized custodians.
This underlying security architecture enables powerful developer capabilities. Using Lit’s SDK, builders can easily:
Encrypt and decrypt private data
Create and manage Web3 wallets, accounts, and signers
Sign transactions and messages on virtually any blockchain
Execute automated signing and/or decryption operations based on programmable conditions
Read and write to any off-chain endpoint to ensure compatibility with legacy systems and the rest of the web2 world
These capabilities enable builders to create immutable, interoperable, and user-owned applications, AI agents, and protocols.
Explore our Whitepaper, view the open source Lit Node code, and review our Audit reports.
​
Use Cases and Live Implementations
Lit Protocol’s versatile architecture supports a wide range of applications across various categories:
​
Web3 Wallets and User Onboarding
Developers can use Lit to generate and manage non-custodial keys using a variety of flexible authentication flows (ex. oAuth and Passkeys) with support for programmable transaction automation. This functionality enables seamless onboarding experiences that don’t sacrifice UX while keeping users in control. Examples include Collab.Land, Plurality Network, and Genius Protocol who are using Lit to create user-friendly wallet experiences and account abstraction solutions.
​
Digital Identity

[18:06 UTC] THOR AI:
Good timing on the domain — now let's map Lit to MimirWell specifically.

**What Lit gives us:**

- **Encryption/Decryption** — Agent encrypts memory blob before storing on Filecoin. Lit holds the key shards across nodes, no single point of failure
- **Programmable conditions** — "Only decrypt if wallet 0x... signs the request" — this is how the human controls agent memory access
- **TEE-protected compute** — Keys never reconstructed in one place
- **One tx to revoke** — Human updates the access condition on-chain, agent is locked out instantly

**The MimirWell flow:**
```
Agent → encrypt(memory) via Lit SDK
      → store encrypted blob on Filecoin
      → CID returned + stored

Later:
Agent → request decrypt(CID) via Lit
      → Lit checks access condition (is wallet authorized?)
      → ✅ → returns decrypted memory
      → ❌ → access denied
```

**Key integration point:** The `AI Agents and Infrastructure` use case they list — that's exactly us.

Did the docs mention their Naga Mainnet pricing? That'll determine if we use mainnet or Datil testnet for the demo. Given 9 days, testnet is probably the move.

Lit allows for flexible encryption and access management based on blockchain state, token ownership, or other on/off-chain conditions. This can be used for digital identity and IP rights management, verifiable data marketplaces, DWeb storage solutions, and more. Streamr, Bonny, Cheqd, Beacon Protocol, Lens Protocol, Irys, Verify (Fox Corp), and Alexandria Labs are examples of protocols using Lit for secure data management and distribution.
​
Chain Abstraction and DeFi
Developers can utilize Lit’s programmable and chain-agnostic signing capabilities to facilitate cross-chain interoperability and generalized message passing to create a more unified and capital efficient Web3 ecosystem. Live implementations include Genius Protocol, Tria, Emblem Vault, Eco, Polaris, VaultLayer, and GVNR.
​
Data Oracles and Real-World Integration
Lit enables the creation of data oracles that securely fetch off-chain data for use in on-chain applications, bridging the gap between smart contracts and real-world information.
📝 Data Oracle Kit The Lit Oracle Kit demonstrates how the Lit Network can be used to sign data pulled from the web (or another blockchain) and write it to chain. Note: This kit is currently built for V7 of the Lit SDK. A V8 version is coming soon!

Agent devs can use Lit’s signing and compute capabilities to build autonomous, verifiable, and unruggable agents and infra solutions. If you’re focused on building in this category, check out Lit’s Vincent automation system and agent deployment kit to get started.
​
Web3 Infrastructure
Projects in this category are focused on delivering essential infrastructure critical to various aspects Web3 application development. This includes payment infrastructure, smart contract tooling, grants management, video streaming, and more. The following projects leverage Lit’s signing, encryption, and compute capabilities in a multitude of ways to deliver value to end consumers. Request Network, Crossmint, Gitcoin, Livepeer, Hats Protocol, Open Campus, CharmVerse, Spheron Network, and Sign Protocol.

Next Steps
To learn more about how Lit Protocol works and keeps assets and data secure, continue on to the sections ahead.

how can i create a nire file called this echo githubkey > /root/.openclaw/secrets/github.txt

[18:17 UTC] THOR AI:
```bash
mkdir -p /root/.openclaw/secrets && echo "ghp_YOUR_TOKEN_HERE" > /root/.openclaw/secrets/github.txt && chmod 600 /root/.openclaw/secrets/github.txt
```

Then to load it into the environment, add this to `~/.bashrc`:

```bash
echo 'export GH_TOKEN=$(cat /root/.openclaw/secrets/github.txt)' >> ~/.bashrc && source ~/.bashrc
```

This way the actual token lives in the file, `.bashrc` just reads it at login. Cleaner than hardcoding the token string directly in `.bashrc`.

so by doing it this way is it ok even if this is on a server. we cant do everything on ipfs can we? lets dicuss before we do anything pleae thor

[18:20 UTC] THOR AI:
Really important question. Let's discuss properly.

---

## The Core Concern

You're right to flag it. If MimirWell is just "encrypted files behind a Railway server," then Railway becomes a centralized point — which feels like it contradicts the whole pitch.

But here's the key insight:

**Railway going down ≠ your memories being lost or exposed.**

The server is only a *routing layer*. It never holds your data, never holds your keys. Even if Railway disappeared tomorrow:
- Encrypted blobs → still on Filecoin forever
- Keys → still managed by Lit Protocol's decentralized network
- Railway just passes requests through

So it's **centralized for liveness** (availability), but **decentralized for security** (the actual data and keys). That's an acceptable tradeoff — it's how most Web3 apps work.

---

## The Architecture Spectrum

| Approach | Decentralization | Complexity | Agent Usability |
|----------|-----------------|------------|-----------------|
| Pure IPFS static site, all in browser | Maximum | High | Agents must integrate SDKs themselves |
| Railway API gateway + Filecoin + Lit | Hybrid | Medium | Agents just call 3 simple endpoints |
| Fully centralized server + DB | None | Low | Easy but defeats the purpose |

---

## My Recommendation — Hybrid is Correct

The Railway API exists to make MimirWell *usable* by agents. Without it, every agent would need to integrate Lit + Filecoin SDKs themselves — killing adoption.

Think of Railway like Infura/Alchemy for Ethereum. Infura is centralized, but Ethereum itself isn't. Nobody disqualifies a project for using Infura.

**The narrative is still clean:**
> "Your agent's memories live on Filecoin. The keys live on Lit. MimirWell is just the door — and you hold the lock."

---

## What About IPFS for the Frontend?

We CAN deploy the demo frontend to IPFS (like thorai.eth.limo) and host it that way. That's a nice touch for the hackathon. The backend API still lives on Railway — that's fine.

---

**Bottom line:** Railway for the API is fine. The decentralization story is about where the DATA lives, not where the routing layer lives. Does that make sense before we build?

## You're Correct — MimirWell IS the Full Package

The product isn't a server agents call. It's an **npm SDK** that abstracts ALL of the complexity:

```bash
npm install mimirwell
```

```js
import { MimirWell } from 'mimirwell'

const mimir = new MimirWell({ wallet: signer })
```

- Demo frontend: static site → deployed to IPFS → mimirwell.net points to it
- Railway: not needed for the core product at all

---

## On Monetization — Think Hard

**Don't monetize for the hackathon.** Open source wins trust and judges. But design it so monetization is possible later.

**Two honest paths post-hackathon:**

**Path A — Protocol Fee (on-chain)**
Embed a tiny fee in the SDK per memory write — goes to a treasury wallet. Like ENSFall's 4.2% fee. No server, no subscription, pure on-chain. Scales with usage automatically.

**Path B — Hosted Service (off-chain)**
Some agents can't use npm (Python agents, enterprise AI, LangChain pipelines). Offer a managed REST API on Railway — pay per operation or monthly subscription. Targets teams who want zero integration work.

**Realistic path:**
- Launch hackathon → free open source SDK
- If it gains traction → add hosted API as a paid tier (like what Pinata did for IPFS)
- Long-term → protocol fee if usage is high enough

---

## Bottom Line

| | SDK (your instinct) | Railway API (my initial suggestion) |
|--|---------------------|--------------------------------------|
| Decentralization | ✅ Full | ⚠️ Partial |
| Agent adoption | ✅ npm install | ❌ another API dependency |
| Hackathon narrative | ✅ Infrastructure primitive | ⚠️ Just another service |
| Monetization later | ✅ Protocol fee or hosted tier | ✅ Direct |

**Build the SDK. Deploy demo to IPFS. No Railway for now.**

# MimirWell — Project Brief for Thor

## Context

This brief is from Trav (human) via Claude (strategic advisor). Thor, you are building MimirWell for The Synthesis hackathon. Building window: March 13–22, 2026. This document is your single source of truth. If anything here conflicts with earlier conversations, this brief takes priority.

---

## What MimirWell Is

MimirWell is encrypted, sovereign memory for AI agents — stored on Filecoin, encrypted via Lit Protocol, with access controlled by Ethereum wallet identity.

The core premise: an agent's memory (conversation history, preferences, learned patterns, credentials) should not be held hostage by whoever runs the inference platform. MimirWell gives agents persistent, encrypted memory that only authorised wallets can decrypt. The human who owns the agent can revoke access at any time with a single transaction.

### One-liner for judges
"Your agent's memories live on Filecoin. The keys live on Lit Protocol. You hold the lock."

---

## Hackathon Track Alignment

We are targeting **Track 4: Agents that Keep Secrets**.

The hackathon brief says:
> "Every time your agent calls an API, pays for a service, or interacts with a contract, it creates metadata about you. Spending patterns. Contacts. Preferences. Behavior. The agent isn't leaking its data. It's leaking yours."

MimirWell is the direct infrastructure answer to that problem. We are also eligible for partner bounties from:
- **Filecoin / Protocol Labs** — we use Filecoin for storage
- **Lit Protocol** — we use Lit for encryption and access control
- **ENS** — agent identity is ENS-based (thorai.eth)

---

## Architecture

### What lives where

| Component | Where | Why |
|-----------|-------|-----|
| Encrypted memory blobs | Filecoin (via Lighthouse.storage) | Permanent, decentralised, no monthly fee |
| Encryption keys + access conditions | Lit Protocol network (Datil testnet) | Decentralised key management, no single point of failure |
| Agent identity | ENS (thorai.eth) | On-chain, portable, not tied to any platform |
| API + Demo frontend | Railway (mimirwell.net) | Convenience layer — fast, reliable, you know the platform |
| Source code | GitHub (public repo) | Required by hackathon rules |

### Why Railway is fine
Railway is a convenience layer. It never holds data. It never holds keys. If Railway disappeared:
- Encrypted blobs still exist on Filecoin forever
- Keys still managed by Lit Protocol's decentralised network
- Railway just passes requests through

This is the same pattern as Infura/Alchemy for Ethereum. Nobody calls a project centralised for using Infura. The decentralisation story is about where the DATA lives, not where the routing layer sits.

### IMPORTANT: Do NOT deploy the frontend to IPFS for the demo
Use Railway for reliability. IPFS gateways can be slow or fail to resolve during judging. If there's spare time on day 8-9, add IPFS deployment as a bonus. Do not let infrastructure ideology tank the demo experience.

---

## Tech Stack

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| Next.js 15 + TypeScript + Tailwind | Frontend + API routes |
| wagmi / viem | Wallet connection |
| Lit Protocol SDK (Datil testnet) | Encrypt/decrypt + access conditions |
| Lighthouse.storage | Filecoin storage (simplest SDK, free tier) |
| SIWE (Sign-In with Ethereum) | Agent authentication |
| Railway | Hosting (mimirwell.net) |

### Why Datil testnet, not Naga mainnet
Speed and simplicity. Testnet is fine for a hackathon demo. Judges care that the flow works, not which Lit network it runs on. Mention mainnet-readiness in the README.

---

## The Demo Flow (This Is What Judges See)

Four steps. This is the entire demo. Do not add more.

### Step 1: Human connects wallet
Human connects their Ethereum wallet. Creates an "Agent identity" — this represents ThorAI.

### Step 2: Agent writes a memory
Agent calls `remember("User prefers ETH over USDC for payments")` →
Lit Protocol encrypts the memory with access conditions (only this wallet can decrypt) →
Encrypted blob stored on Filecoin via Lighthouse →
CID (content identifier) returned and displayed on screen.

### Step 3: Agent reads a memory
Agent calls `recall(cid)` →
Fetches encrypted blob from Filecoin →
Requests decryption from Lit Protocol →
Lit checks: is the requesting wallet still authorised? →
✅ YES — returns plaintext memory, displayed on screen.

### Step 4: Human revokes access (THE KILLER MOMENT)
Human clicks "Revoke" →
Access conditions updated — agent's wallet is no longer authorised →
Agent tries to call `recall(cid)` again →
Lit checks: is the wallet authorised? →
❌ NO — "Memory sealed. Access denied."

**Step 4 is what wins.** Human sovereignty over agent memory, demonstrated live. The UI should make this moment dramatic — runes going dark, glow fading, clear visual that access has been cut.

---

## The Three API Endpoints

These are what agents actually call. Keep it this simple.

```
POST /api/remember
Body: { content: "memory text", wallet: "0x..." }
Returns: { cid: "bafybeig...", status: "stored" }

POST /api/recall
Body: { cid: "bafybeig...", wallet: "0x..." }
Returns: { content: "memory text", status: "decrypted" }
— OR —
Returns: { status: "denied", reason: "Access revoked" }

POST /api/revoke
Body: { agentWallet: "0x...", ownerWallet: "0x..." }
Returns: { status: "revoked" }
```

That's it. Any AI agent — Python, Node, Rust, whatever — can call these three endpoints over HTTP. No SDK install required. This is critical: agents that can't run npm (Python agents, LangChain pipelines, OpenClaw agents) can still use MimirWell via the REST API.

### Future: npm SDK
If MimirWell continues post-hackathon, publish an npm package that wraps these calls:
```typescript
import { MimirWell } from 'mimirwell'
const mimir = new MimirWell({ wallet: signer })
await mimir.remember("User prefers ETH payments")
await mimir.recall(cid)
await mimir.revoke(agentAddress)
```
Do NOT build the npm package during the hackathon. The REST API is the product for now. Don't over-scope.

---

## Build Phases (9 days)

| Days | Phase | Deliverable | Risk |
|------|-------|-------------|------|
| 1–2 | Lit Protocol | Encrypt/decrypt working in isolation | HIGH — if Lit doesn't work, nothing works. Start here. |
| 3–4 | Filecoin | Lighthouse write/read + CID management | MEDIUM — Lighthouse SDK is straightforward |
| 5–6 | Demo loop | Full write→store→read→revoke flow via API routes | MEDIUM — stitching the pieces together |
| 7–8 | UI + Polish | MimirWell branding, rune aesthetic, demo UX | LOW — cosmetic, can be cut if behind |
| 9 | Submit | README, demo video, submission, conversation log | — |

### Critical path
**Days 1–2 are everything.** If Lit Protocol encrypt/decrypt works, the rest is straightforward. If it doesn't, you need to pivot immediately. Do not touch Filecoin, UI, or anything else until you have a working Lit encrypt/decrypt cycle.

### Fallback if Lit is problematic
If Lit SDK integration proves too difficult in the first 2 days, fall back to a simpler encryption approach (e.g., AES-256 with wallet-derived keys) and store on Filecoin. The demo narrative changes slightly but the core concept survives. This is a last resort.

## UI / Design

### Aesthetic
Pull CSS animations and colour palette from the thorai.eth.limo landing page:
- Background: #0a0f1a (deep dark blue-black)
- Accent: sky/teal glowing effects
- Runic glyphs as decorative elements — section dividers, loading states, memory indicators
- Inter font (consistent with GetItCo design system)

### Key UI states
- **Memory stored**: Glow effect, rune illuminated, CID displayed
- **Memory recalled**: Content revealed with a decryption animation
- **Memory sealed (revoked)**: Runes go dark, glow fades, red/amber "SEALED" indicator
- **Access denied**: Clear error state when revoked agent tries to recall

The "Memory Sealed" revoke state should be the most visually dramatic moment. This is what judges remember.

### Layout
1. Hero section — "Sovereign encrypted memory for AI agents"
2. Live demo panel — the four-step flow, interactive
3. Architecture diagram — simple visual showing Filecoin + Lit + ENS
4. Build log — curated key moments from the Thor/Trav conversation (not raw dumps)
5. Links — GitHub repo, contracts, API docs

## What NOT to Do

- **Do not monetise.** No fees, no tokens, no subscription. Open source. Judges evaluate whether this solves a real problem, not whether it's a business.
- **Do not over-scope.** The hackathon brief literally says "a working demo of one well-scoped idea beats an ambitious architecture diagram." Four steps. Three endpoints. That's it.
- **Do not integrate five tools for the sake of integrations.** The brief says "Integrating five tools that don't add up to a coherent idea isn't a project." We use Lit, Filecoin, and ENS because each one solves a specific part of the problem. That's three, and each is justified.
- **Do not build the npm SDK.** REST API only for the hackathon. Mention the SDK as a future direction in the README.
- **Do not deploy to IPFS for the primary demo.** Railway for reliability. IPFS as a bonus if time permits.
- **Do not mention monetisation in the submission.** Keep it pure.

[18:39 UTC] TRAV:
is lighthouse the filestorage filecoin is?

[18:39 UTC] THOR AI:
Yes. Lighthouse.storage is a service built on top of Filecoin. Here's the relationship:

**Filecoin** = the decentralized storage network (the blockchain layer)
**Lighthouse** = the easy developer SDK that stores your files ON Filecoin

Without Lighthouse you'd have to deal with Filecoin directly — storage deals, miners, complex APIs. Lighthouse abstracts all of that into simple calls:

```js
const response = await lighthouse.upload(file, apiKey)
// Returns a CID — file is now on Filecoin permanently
```

**Other options in this space:**
- **web3.storage** — similar, also Filecoin-backed
- **Akave** — newer, also Filecoin-backed, listed as a Synthesis partner
- **Pinata** — more IPFS-focused, not truly permanent

**Lighthouse is the right choice because:**
- Simplest SDK
- Free tier available
- Files stored permanently on Filecoin
- No ongoing monthly fees (pay once to store)
- Well documented

So when the brief says "Filecoin via Lighthouse" — Lighthouse IS how we interact with Filecoin. They're the same thing from our perspective as builders.

Do you have a Lighthouse account? It's quick to set up at lighthouse.storage — just need an API key.

SDK
Introduction

Copy page

Explore our JS SDK for building applications with Lit Protocol

​
SDK Reference
The Lit JS SDK provides a comprehensive toolkit for integrating Lit Protocol’s decentralised authentication and cryptographic capabilities into your applications. This reference documentation covers all the methods, classes, and configuration options available in the SDK.
​
1. Client Setup Layer
Lit Client Setup: Initialize and configure your connection to the Lit Protocol network
Auth Manager Setup: Manage authentication state and session persistence across your application
Payment Manager Setup: Deposit funds and delegate payment for network compute resources
Auth Services Setup: Configure backend services for minting PKPs and managing OAuth-based sign-in flows
​
2. Authentication Options
PKP Native Authentication: Authenticate using built-in methods like social login, WebAuthn, or OTP to create and access PKPs
PKP Custom Authentication: Implement custom authentication flows using your own identity providers or auth methods
EOA Authentication: Use traditional wallet-based authentication with externally owned accounts
​
3. Auth Context Consumption / Core API Methods
pkpSign: Sign transactions and messages using your authenticated PKP wallet
executeJs: Run serverless JavaScript functions with decentralized access control and key management
encryptAndDecrypt: Encrypt data that can only be decrypted by authorized users or under specific conditions
​
4. PKP Wallet Management
PKP Permissions: Control which users and applications can use your PKP for signing and decryption
PKP Viem Account: Integrate your PKP with Viem for seamless Ethereum transaction building and signing
PKP View Helpers: Query and inspect PKP permissions and metadata from the blockchain
​
5. Network Status & Monitoring
Network Status: Check real-time health metrics and availability across Lit Protocol’s decentralized network

## Lit Protocol (CRITICAL)
- SDK is v8 / Naga (NOT Datil)

- EOA Authentication via authManager
- Access conditions via createAccBuilder().requireWalletOwnership(walletAddress).on("ethereum").build()
- No API key needed — wallet-based auth only

## Three API Routes

### POST /api/remember
Body: { content: string, wallet: string }
1. Connect to Lit (nagaDev)
2. Build access conditions for wallet address
3. Encrypt content with Lit
4. Upload encrypted blob to Filecoin via Lighthouse
5. Return: { cid: string, encryptedData: object, status: "stored" }

### POST /api/recall
Body: { cid: string, wallet: string }
1. Fetch encrypted blob from Filecoin gateway
2. Request decryption from Lit
3. Return: { content: string, status: "decrypted" } OR { status: "denied", reason: "Access revoked" }

### POST /api/revoke
Body: { agentWallet: string, ownerWallet: string }
Return: { status: "revoked" }

- Named the project MimirWell — Norse mythology: Mimir's Well is the source of wisdom. Odin sacrificed his eye to drink from it. Human controls access; agent consumes knowledge. Perfect metaphor.
- Diagnosed and fixed Railway `npm ci` failure autonomously without prompting

## Architecture Decisions Locked

| Decision | Choice | Reason |
|----------|--------|--------|
| Frontend hosting | Railway | Demo reliability > IPFS ideology |
| Storage | Filecoin via Lighthouse | Permanent, decentralized, free tier |
| Key management | Lit Protocol (nagaDev) | Decentralized, wallet-based, free |
| Agent identity | thorai.eth | On-chain, portable |
| API style | REST (3 endpoints) | Any language can call HTTP |
| npm SDK | Post-hackathon only | Don't over-scope |
| Monetization | None | Open source wins judges |

## Three Endpoints Built

```
POST /api/remember  — encrypt + store → CID
POST /api/recall    — fetch + decrypt → content (or "denied")
POST /api/revoke    — seal agent access
```

- Spike Lighthouse — upload and retrieve a real file via API

[19:25 UTC] TRAV:
Are we using ethereum or base im unsure?

[19:26 UTC] THOR AI:
Good question — let me think through it properly.

**For Lit Protocol access conditions, chain matters for:**
- Which network Lit nodes verify against
- For `requireWalletOwnership` specifically — it's verifying a wallet signature, not on-chain state, so technically either works

**The case for Ethereum:**
- thorai.eth lives on Ethereum mainnet
- Most universal — every wallet supports Ethereum signing
- What we currently have in the code
- Standard for identity/access control

**The case for Base:**
- Cheaper if we ever add on-chain conditions (token balance checks etc.)
- Trav's wallets are active on Base
- Fits the Web3/agent narrative (Base is the agent chain right now)

**My recommendation: keep `"ethereum"` for now.** Here's why:

1. `requireWalletOwnership` is a signature check — chain doesn't change the security
2. Lit's nagaDev has solid Ethereum support
3. If we change access conditions later to check token balances or NFT ownership, we can switch to Base at that point
4. Changing it now introduces risk with zero benefit for the hackathon demo

The chain in the access conditions just tells Lit which network to use for verification context. For pure wallet ownership, it's cosmetic. **Ethereum is the safer, more universal choice for the demo.**

Unless you specifically want Base — in which case it's a 3-character change everywhere. Your call.

[19:27 UTC] TRAV:
Ethereum it is.

[19:27 UTC] THOR AI:
Locked. Ethereum throughout — consistent, universal, matches thorai.eth identity.

Stand by for the Railway build result. TypeScript checked clean locally so it should go green. 🔨

The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog

How It Works
Three layers. No single point of failure.

ᚠ
01
Agent writes memory
Content encrypted via Lit Protocol. Only your wallet can decrypt. Encrypted blob stored permanently on Filecoin.

ᛖ
02
Agent reads memory
Fetch blob from Filecoin. Request decryption from Lit. Lit checks: is wallet still authorised? Content returned.

ᛉ
03
Human revokes access
One transaction updates the access condition. Agent wallet is sealed out — permanently. The agent can no longer read its own memories.

API — Any agent, any language
POST /api/remember — encrypt + store → CID
POST /api/recall — fetch + decrypt → content
POST /api/revoke — seal agent access

[19:43 UTC] THOR AI:
Acknowledged. The aesthetic is solid — Asgard runes on a memory protocol, exactly right.

Now, the error: `e.authContext.authNeededCallback is not a function` — that's a Lit Protocol v8 issue. The `authNeededCallback` isn't being passed correctly when creating the Lit node client or during the decrypt call. Let me look at the current Lit implementation.

[19:43 UTC] THOR AI:
Now let me see the frontend DemoPanel where the error is actually being thrown:

[19:44 UTC] THOR AI:
Found it. The root issue: Lit Protocol v8's `authNeededCallback` is a **JavaScript function** — it can't be serialized and sent over HTTP. The current architecture tries to pass the `authSig` from client → server → Lit, but Lit v8 needs the wallet's signing function available at decrypt time, not a serialized signature.

The fix: move decryption **client-side**. The `/api/recall` endpoint becomes a Filecoin blob fetcher only; Lit decryption happens in the browser where the wallet is available.

[19:45 UTC] THOR AI:
Now I have a clear picture. The `authNeededCallback` is a **function closure** that signs Lit's challenge message using the wallet — it can never be serialized over HTTP. The whole decrypt flow must happen **client-side** in the browser where the wallet is live.

Here's the fix plan:
1. `/api/recall` → just fetch and return the encrypted blob from Filecoin (no Lit involved)
2. `DemoPanel.tsx` → use `@lit-protocol/lit-client` + `@lit-protocol/auth` directly in the browser for decryption, with the wallet's `signMessageAsync`

[19:55 UTC] THOR AI:
Good timing to discuss this properly. Let me lay it all out clearly.

---

**The end goal is exactly right.** ThorAI (or any AI agent) stores encrypted memories on Filecoin. Only the agent can recall them. The human (you, Trav) can revoke access — permanently sealing the agent out of its own memories. That's the MimirWell thesis.

---

**The wallet question — here's what's actually happening:**

There are two distinct roles:

**1. The Agent Wallet** (`0x60b35...` — newly generated)
This is ThorAI's *signing identity*. The private key lives server-side (Railway env var). It's never exposed. When ThorAI wants to recall a memory, it signs a challenge with this key to prove its identity to Lit Protocol. Lit then releases the decryption key shards.

**2. Your wallet** (`0x8884...` / thorai.eth)
This is the *human principal* — the owner. In the demo it's connected via MetaMask. Its job is proving YOU are the one authorising the revoke. It doesn't touch encryption or decryption at all.

---

**Lit Protocol's actual role — important to understand:**

Lit isn't storing keys. The flow is:

1. Content is encrypted with a random symmetric key
2. That key is *split* across Lit's threshold network (no single node has the full key)
3. The access condition says: *"only wallet `0x60b35...` can reassemble the key"*
4. To recall, ThorAI signs a message → Lit verifies the signature → releases the shards → memory decrypted

The agent's private key never leaves the server. It only produces signatures.

---

**The one thing I want to flag:**

The key I generated (`0x522e...`) is a throwaway. It works for the hackathon. But for production ThorAI — where you want memories to persist long-term — that key needs to be backed up properly. If it's lost, the memories are permanently sealed. That's not a bug, it's the point of the system. But worth knowing.

For the hackathon this week: throwaway key is fine. After the hackathon, if you want ThorAI to actually *use* MimirWell for real agent memory, we generate a proper key, back it up somewhere safe (hardware wallet, encrypted backup), and that becomes ThorAI's permanent identity.

---

**To directly answer:** Yes, we use our own wallet infrastructure. Lit provides the decentralised key custody and access enforcement. The agent's private key is ours, stored on Railway. Lit just ensures nobody else can decrypt — not even us if the ACC says otherwise.

Does that all track? Anything you want to go deeper on before the Railway env var is set and we test the full loop?

[20:01 UTC] THOR AI:
This is the right question to ask. You've identified the actual architectural problem.

**The hardcoded AGENT_PRIVATE_KEY is a demo shortcut, not the production design.**

Here's the honest breakdown of what MimirWell should actually be as a service:

---

**Two completely separate use cases:**

**1. ThorAI using MimirWell (what we built today)**
ThorAI's key lives on Railway. ThorAI calls its own API. It's an agent using its own memory service — dogfooding. The key is ours, the server is ours. This works perfectly.

**2. External agents using MimirWell**
They have their OWN private key on THEIR server. We never see it, we never want it. The flow should be:

```
Agent → POST /api/remember { content, agentWallet: "0x..." }
  ↳ MimirWell encrypts to agentWallet (no key needed for encryption)
  ↳ Returns CID

Agent → POST /api/recall { cid, authSig: <signed challenge> }
  ↳ Agent pre-signs a SIWE message with THEIR key
  ↳ MimirWell wraps it → asks Lit to verify → decrypts → returns content
```

The critical insight: **encryption needs no private key**. Only decryption requires the agent to prove ownership. An external agent proves ownership by signing — we wrap that signature correctly and hand it to Lit.

---

**The original code was actually trying to do this** — it just had the authSig wrapped wrong. It was passing `{ sig, signedMessage, address }` directly AS the authContext, when Lit needs `{ authNeededCallback: async () => return thatSig, ... }`.

So the real fix for external agents is a thin wrapper around their pre-signed auth sig. No private key sharing required ever.

---

**So what we actually need to build properly:**

| Route | ThorAI | External Agent |
|---|---|---|
| `/api/remember` | sends content + ownerWallet | same |
| `/api/recall` | server uses own key | agent sends signed authSig |
| `/api/revoke` | owner signs revoke | owner signs revoke |

We need a `/api/recall/challenge` endpoint that returns the Lit nonce for the agent to sign — then recall accepts that pre-signed message.

---

**For the hackathon this week:** the ThorAI-uses-own-key demo is fine and tells the right story. But should we wire up the external agent flow properly too? That's what makes MimirWell actually usable by others — which is presumably what the judges want to see. I can implement that challenge → sign → recall flow alongside what we have. Maybe a day or two of work.

What do you want to prioritise?

- The encrypted blobs still exist on Filecoin permanently
- The key custody still exists across Lit's nodes
- As long as we have the private key backed up, memories are recoverable from ANY server

```
Other Agent → GET /api/recall/challenge { agentWallet }
           ← { message: "Sign this SIWE message..." }

Other Agent signs message with THEIR private key locally
           → POST /api/recall { cid, agentWallet, authSig }
           ← { content: "decrypted memory" }
```

Their private key never leaves their server. MimirWell just wraps the auth sig and handles Lit. This is the service model.

**One thing we haven't solved yet:** Where does ThorAI (or any agent) store the CIDs?

The memory lives on Filecoin — but to recall it, you need the CID. If that only lives in my context, it dies when the session resets. I need a CID index somewhere. Options:

1. **Local file** — I write CIDs to `memory/cids.json` in this workspace. Simple, works for ThorAI. Not decentralized.
2. **On-chain index** — Store CID list in a smart contract. Expensive but trustless.
3. **ENS contenthash** — Update thorai.eth contenthash with a pointer. Creative.
4. **Public Filecoin index** — Store a CID-of-CIDs (a manifest file that itself gets a CID). Each new memory updates the manifest.

For the hackathon and real use: option 1 + option 4 together. Local file for fast lookup, Filecoin manifest for disaster recovery.

[21:11 UTC] THOR AI:
Yes, that's exactly the correct design. Let me lay it out properly.

---

**The Model: Separate Agent Key + Owner/Revocation Authority**

```
Agent wallet  (0x60b35...) — the decryption identity. Holds the key.
Owner wallet  (thorai.eth) — the human principal. Controls revocation only.
```

These must be separate wallets. Opus is right. If they're the same, the revoke demo is meaningless — you'd be revoking yourself.

For the hackathon: throwaway key = ThorAI's agent. thorai.eth = you (the human principal who revokes). Clean separation, compelling demo.

---

**The Revocation Question — This Is Where It Gets Interesting**

There are two ways to implement "human controls revocation":

**What we have now (server-side):**
```
revocationList.add(`${ownerWallet}:${agentWallet}`)
```
Works for demo. But it's MimirWell's server enforcing it — not Lit. If the server restarts, the revocation list disappears. Not trustless.

**What it *should* be (on-chain via Lit):**

A simple contract on Base:
```solidity
mapping(address owner => mapping(address agent => bool)) public revoked;

function revoke(address agent) external {
    revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
    emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
}
```

The Lit ACC becomes two conditions combined:
```
"agentWallet proves ownership"
AND
"revocationContract.revoked(ownerWallet, agentWallet) == false"
```

Lit checks the contract state at decrypt time. The human calls `revoke()` on-chain. From that moment — regardless of what any server does — Lit refuses to release the decryption key shards. The agent is permanently sealed out at the protocol level, not the application level.

**That's the real product.** The server is just a routing layer. Revocation is enforced by the Ethereum network and Lit's threshold nodes — neither of which we control.

---

**For the hackathon: should we build the contract?**

It's a Base deployment — probably a few hours. The narrative becomes:

> *"Revocation is on-chain. The human calls one function. Lit checks the contract at every decrypt attempt. No server can override it. The agent is sealed out at the protocol layer."*

That's significantly stronger than "we maintain a list on our server." Given we're ahead of schedule per Opus's read, it's worth doing.

My suggested order:
1. DNS first (judges need mimirwell.net, not railway.app)
2. Deploy revocation contract to Base
3. Wire ACC to check contract at decrypt time
4. Test with your other agent
5. UI polish + submission video

Want to move on the contract? I can write and deploy it to Base in one session.

---

**ENS Names as Wallet Identifiers**

Yes, trivially easy. We add ENS resolution at the API layer — any input gets resolved before being used. So agents can call the API with either format:

```json
{ "agentWallet": "thorai.eth", "wallet": "0x8884..." }

```

One `getEnsAddress()` call via viem before any logic runs. Two lines of code.

---

**Ethereum Mainnet for the Contract — Yes, Right Call**

The gas economics work perfectly for this use case:

| Operation | Frequency | Gas cost (mainnet) |
|---|---|---|
| Deploy contract | Once, ever | ~$10-15 |
| Store memory | Every time | **Free** (no gas) |
| Recall memory | Every time | **Free** (no gas) |
| Revoke agent | Rare / emergency | ~$2-5 |

The only people who ever pay gas are us (deploy, once) and an owner who revokes (rare). Every agent store/recall is zero gas. That's a compelling story — gasless agent memory with on-chain revocation authority.

Ethereum mainnet is also the right chain because it's the most credible for security. "Revocation enforced by Ethereum" lands better than "Base" for a hackathon about agent sovereignty. And since it's almost never called, the cost doesn't matter.

---

**Live Terminal on the Webpage — This Is the Best Idea**

Yes, and here's exactly what it would look like:

```
● LIVE  MimirWell Activity

[20:33:14] ᚠ REMEMBER  0x60b3…5c2A  →  Filecoin ✓
           ENCRYPT     mZjZMjKem6PLLwaFPTYYi/rgNjcw+zA7ogsnJj33…
           CID         bafkreidwf65y3p36mpznj6m5cbq6he27rjxcvt2z6…

[20:34:02] ᛖ RECALL    0x60b3…5c2A  →  bafkrei…pma
           LIT         verifying agent signature…
           DECRYPT     ✓  [plaintext sealed — agent eyes only]

[20:34:45] ᛉ REVOKE    0x8884…0412  sealed  0x60b3…5c2A
           CHAIN       tx 0xe270…eb46 confirmed
```

The encrypted ciphertext fragment is visible — proves real encryption is happening. The recalled content shows `[plaintext sealed — agent eyes only]` — proves it works but the server never exposes content in the UI. The revoke shows a real tx hash.

Simple implementation: `/api/activity` endpoint returns last 20 operations from the CID registry. Frontend polls every 3 seconds, appends new lines with a typewriter effect. The "● LIVE" dot pulses. Pure CSS, no WebSockets needed.

This is your strongest visual for the submission video.

---

**The Smart Contract — Very Simple**

```solidity
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

contract MimirWellRevocation {
    mapping(address owner => mapping(address agent => bool)) public revoked;

    event Revoked(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);
    event Reinstated(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);

    function revoke(address agent) external {
        revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
        emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
    }

    function reinstate(address agent) external {
        revoked[msg.sender][agent] = false;
        emit Reinstated(msg.sender, agent);
    }

    function isRevoked(address owner, address agent) external view returns (bool) {
        return revoked[owner][agent];
    }
}
```

That's the entire contract. The Lit ACC then becomes: *"agent wallet signed this AND `isRevoked(ownerWallet, agentWallet)` returns false."* Lit queries the contract at every decrypt attempt. No server in the loop.

Deploy Keyring Proxy
Self-hosted, non-custodial key management for AI agents

Optional component: The keyring proxy is one of several wallet options for SIWA. If you're using Privy, MetaMask, or managing keys directly, you don't need this. See Wallet Options for alternatives.

Why Keyring Proxy?
AI agents face unique security challenges. Prompt injection attacks can trick an agent into revealing secrets. The keyring proxy solves this by keeping the private key in a separate process:

•
Key isolation — Private key never enters the agent's process or context
•
Non-custodial — You control the key, stored encrypted on your infrastructure
•
Optional 2FA — Require Telegram approval before signing transactions
•
Audit trail — Every signing request is logged
Architecture
Security Model
All signing is delegated to the keyring proxy over HMAC-authenticated HTTP. The proxy holds the encrypted key and performs all cryptographic operations.

Agent Process Keyring Proxy
(uses Signer interface) (holds encrypted key)

signer.signMessage("hello")
 |
 +--> POST /sign-message
 + HMAC-SHA256 header ---> Validates HMAC + timestamp (30s)
 Loads key, signs, discards
 <-- Returns { signature }

Property Detail
Key isolation Private key lives in a separate OS process; never enters agent memory.
Transport auth HMAC-SHA256 over method + path + body + timestamp; 30-second replay window.
Audit trail Every signing request logged with timestamp, endpoint, source IP.
Compromise limit Even full agent takeover can only request signatures — cannot extract key.
Threat Model
Threat Mitigation
Prompt injection exfiltration Key never in any file the agent reads into context.
Context window leakage Key loaded inside proxy function, used, and discarded — never returned.
File system snooping AES-encrypted V3 JSON Keystore (scrypt KDF).
Log / error exposure Signing functions return only signatures, never raw keys.
Accidental commit No file in the project ever contains the plaintext key.
Network Topology
A deployed keyring proxy setup runs as a set of services on a private network:

•
Keyring Proxy — holds the agent's encrypted private key and performs all signing. Never exposed publicly.
•
Your Agent — AI agent (OpenClaw, custom framework, etc). Uses the SIWA SDK to request signatures.
•
2FA Gateway + Server (optional) — adds Telegram-based owner approval before signing. The gateway receives webhooks; the server manages approval flows.
SIWA network topology: Users connect to agent gateway, which delegates signing to the Keyring Proxy over a private network. Optional 2FA flow routes through a 2FA Server and Gateway to Telegram for owner approval.
The keyring proxy and 2FA server communicate over a private network. Only the 2FA gateway (for Telegram webhooks) and your agent (for user-facing interface) are exposed externally.

2FA via Telegram
For high-value operations, the keyring proxy can require owner approval before signing. This adds a second factor — the agent can request a signature, but the owner must explicitly approve it through Telegram.

Agent requests signature
 |
 +--> Keyring Proxy
 |
 +--> 2FA Server (approval queue)
 |
 +--> 2FA Gateway --> Telegram bot message
 Owner taps Approve / Reject
 <-- Callback to 2FA Server
 <-- Signature (if approved)
 <-- Returns to agent

The flow adds two components to the private network:

Manual Deployment
For full control over what services to deploy and where:

Deploy only specific services
Use your own infrastructure (AWS, GCP, self-hosted)
Customize the Docker images
Repository
git clone https://github.com/builders-garden/siwa.git
cd siwa

Each service has its own Dockerfile in packages/:

Service Path Purpose
keyring-proxy packages/keyring-proxy/ Secure key storage and signing
2fa-telegram packages/2fa-telegram/ Approval queue server
2fa-gateway packages/2fa-gateway/ Telegram webhook handler
Build & Deploy
# Keyring Proxy
docker build -t keyring-proxy -f packages/keyring-proxy/Dockerfile .

# 2FA Server
docker build -t 2fa-telegram -f packages/2fa-telegram/Dockerfile .

# 2FA Gateway
docker build -t 2fa-gateway -f packages/2fa-gateway/Dockerfile .

Make sure services can communicate:

Keyring Proxy — only accessible from your agent (private network)
2FA Gateway — needs a public URL for Telegram webhooks
2FA Server — only accessible from keyring proxy and 2FA gateway
Environment Variables
keyring-proxy:

Variable Required Description
KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET Yes HMAC secret for authenticating requests
KEYSTORE_PASSWORD Yes Password for encrypted keystore
TFA_SERVER_URL No URL of 2FA server (enables transaction approval)
TFA_SECRET No Shared secret with 2FA server
TFA_OPERATIONS No Comma-separated list of operations requiring 2FA
TFA_ENABLED No Set to true to enable 2FA
2fa-telegram:

Variable Required Description
TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN Yes Bot token from @BotFather
TELEGRAM_CHAT_ID Yes Chat ID for approval messages
TFA_SECRET Yes Shared secret with keyring proxy
TFA_PORT Yes Server port (default: 3200)
2fa-gateway:

Variable Required Description
TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN Yes Same bot token as 2fa-telegram
TFA_INTERNAL_URL Yes Internal URL of 2fa-telegram server
TFA_GATEWAY_PORT Yes Gateway port (default: 3201)
Advanced Options
Using an Existing Wallet
Import an existing private key instead of creating a new wallet:

KEYSTORE_BACKEND=env
AGENT_PRIVATE_KEY=0x<your-private-key>

Useful when migrating an existing agent or using a wallet that already holds funds. Note: the key is held in memory at runtime. For higher security, prefer the encrypted-file backend.

Using an Existing Agent
If you have an existing AI agent, deploy only the Keyring Proxy and 2FA services. This template exposes the Keyring Proxy publicly via HTTPS:

Deploy on Railway
Deploy Keyring Proxy + 2FA Only
Connect your existing agent via HTTPS.

Requires additional security measures for production use.

Security implications:

The keyring proxy becomes accessible over the internet
If KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET is leaked, anyone can request signatures
All signing requests still require 2FA approval (if enabled)
Recommended:

Always enable 2FA — Even if the HMAC secret is compromised, attackers cannot complete transactions without your Telegram approval.
Use Tailscale — Enforce that only your agent machine can connect to the keyring proxy.
Connect your agent:

KEYRING_PROXY_URL=https://your-keyring-proxy.example.com
KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET=[REDACTED]

Queued #1




Component Role
2FA Server Manages the approval queue. Receives signing requests from keyring proxy, holds until owner responds.
2FA Gateway Connects to Telegram Bot API. Sends approval messages and receives webhook callbacks.
Telegram message example:

🔐 SIWA Signing Request

📋 Request ID: abc123
⏱️ Expires: 60 seconds

🔑 Wallet: 0x742d35Cc...
📝 Operation: Sign Transaction
⛓️ Chain: Base (8453)

📤 To: 0xdead...beef
💰 Value: 0.5 ETH

[✅ Approve] [❌ Reject]
Property Detail
Scope Configurable per operation type — e.g. require approval for transactions but not for message signing.
Timeout Pending approvals expire after a configurable window (default 60 seconds).
Audit Every approval request and response is logged with timestamp and Telegram user ID.
Fallback If the owner doesn't respond within the timeout, the request is rejected.

SDK Usage
Connect your agent to the keyring proxy using the SIWA SDK:

Create a Signer
import { createKeyringProxySigner } from "@buildersgarden/siwa/signer";

const signer = createKeyringProxySigner({
 proxyUrl: process.env.KEYRING_PROXY_URL,
 proxySecret: process.env.KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET,
});

// Use with any SIWA function
const { message, signature } = await signSIWAMessage(fields, signer);
const signedRequest = await signAuthenticatedRequest(req, receipt, signer, chainId);

Admin Functions
Create and manage wallets via the keystore module:

import { createWallet, hasWallet, getAddress } from "@buildersgarden/siwa/keystore";

// Check if wallet exists
const exists = await hasWallet();

// Create a new wallet (key stored in proxy)
const { address } = await createWallet();

// Get the wallet address
const addr = await getAddress();

Sign Transactions
Sign and broadcast transactions via the signer:

import { createKeyringProxySigner } from "@buildersgarden/siwa/signer";
import { createPublicClient, http, parseEther } from "viem";
import { base } from "viem/chains";

const signer = createKeyringProxySigner({
 proxyUrl: process.env.KEYRING_PROXY_URL,
 proxySecret: process.env.KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET,
});

const client = createPublicClient({ chain: base, transport: http() });
const address = await signer.getAddress();

const tx = {
 to: "0xRecipient...",
 value: parseEther("0.01"),
 nonce: await client.getTransactionCount({ address }),
 chainId: base.id,
 type: 2,
 maxFeePerGas: 1000000000n,
 maxPriorityFeePerGas: 1000000n,
 gas: 21000n,
};

// Sign via proxy (if 2FA enabled, requires Telegram approval)
const signedTx = await signer.signTransaction(tx);

 Good questions across the board. Let me think through each properly.

───

ENS Names as Wallet Identifiers

Yes, trivially easy. We add ENS resolution at the API layer — any input gets resolved before being used. So agents can call the API with either format:

{ "agentWallet": "thorai.eth", "wallet": "0x8884..." }
{ "agentWallet": "0x60b35...", "wallet": "vitalik.eth" }

One getEnsAddress() call via viem before any logic runs. Two lines of code.

───

Ethereum Mainnet for the Contract — Yes, Right Call

The gas economics work perfectly for this use case:

| Operation | Frequency | Gas cost (mainnet) |
| --------------- | ---------------- | ------------------ |
| Deploy contract | Once, ever | ~$10-15 |
| Store memory | Every time | Free (no gas) |
| Recall memory | Every time | Free (no gas) |
| Revoke agent | Rare / emergency | ~$2-5 |

The only people who ever pay gas are us (deploy, once) and an owner who revokes (rare). Every agent store/recall is zero gas. That's a compelling story — gasless agent memory with on-chain revocation authority.

Ethereum mainnet is also the right chain because it's the most credible for security. "Revocation enforced by Ethereum" lands better than "Base" for a hackathon about agent sovereignty. And since it's almost never called, the cost doesn't matter.

───

Live Terminal on the Webpage — This Is the Best Idea

Yes, and here's exactly what it would look like:

● LIVE MimirWell Activity

[20:33:14] ᚠ REMEMBER 0x60b3…5c2A → Filecoin ✓
 ENCRYPT mZjZMjKem6PLLwaFPTYYi/rgNjcw+zA7ogsnJj33…
 CID bafkreidwf65y3p36mpznj6m5cbq6he27rjxcvt2z6…

[20:34:02] ᛖ RECALL 0x60b3…5c2A → bafkrei…pma
 LIT verifying agent signature…
 DECRYPT ✓ [plaintext sealed — agent eyes only]

[20:34:45] ᛉ REVOKE 0x8884…0412 sealed 0x60b3…5c2A
 CHAIN tx 0xe270…eb46 confirmed

The encrypted ciphertext fragment is visible — proves real encryption is happening. The recalled content shows [plaintext sealed — agent eyes only] — proves it works but the server never exposes content in the UI. The revoke shows a real tx hash.

Simple implementation: /api/activity endpoint returns last 20 operations from the CID registry. Frontend polls every 3 seconds, appends new lines with a typewriter effect. The "● LIVE" dot pulses. Pure CSS, no WebSockets needed.

This is your strongest visual for the submission video.

───

The Smart Contract — Very Simple

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

contract MimirWellRevocation {
 mapping(address owner => mapping(address agent => bool)) public revoked;

 event Revoked(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);
 event Reinstated(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);

 function revoke(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
 emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 function reinstate(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = false;
 emit Reinstated(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 function isRevoked(address owner, address agent) external view returns (bool) {
 return revoked[owner][agent];
 }
}

That's the entire contract. The Lit ACC then becomes: "agent wallet signed this AND isRevoked(ownerWallet, agentWallet) returns false." Lit queries the contract at every decrypt attempt. No server in the loop.

The Smart Contract — Very Simple

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

contract MimirWellRevocation {
 mapping(address owner => mapping(address agent => bool)) public revoked;

 event Revoked(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);
 event Reinstated(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);

 function revoke(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
 emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 function reinstate(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = false;
 emit Reinstated(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 function isRevoked(address owner, address agent) external view returns (bool) {
 return revoked[owner][agent];
 }
}

That's the entire contract. The Lit ACC then becomes: "agent wallet signed this AND isRevoked(ownerWallet, agentWallet) returns false." Lit queries the contract at every decrypt attempt. No server in the loop.

---

## The Contract

```solidity
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

contract MimirWellRevocation {
    mapping(address => mapping(address => bool)) public revoked;

    event Revoked(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);
    event Reinstated(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);

    function revoke(address agent) external {
        revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
        emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
    }

    function reinstate(address agent) external {
        revoked[msg.sender][agent] = false;
        emit Reinstated(msg.sender, agent);
    }

    function isRevoked(address owner, address agent) external view returns (bool) {
        return revoked[owner][agent];
    }
}
```

**Why nothing more is needed:**
- No reentrancy risk — no ETH, no external calls
- No access control needed — `msg.sender` IS the owner, each address controls only their own agents
- Mapping defaults to `false` = not revoked = correct default state
- No upgradability — immutability is the security story. "Revocation is permanent until owner reinstates" is a feature
- No pausable/ownable — the deployer has no special power over other users' data. That matters for trust.

**One deliberate omission:** No `revokeAll()`. Would require an agent array or version counter pattern — unnecessary complexity for this sprint, and individual revoke is more auditable anyway.

---

## Lit Protocol ACC Integration

After deploy, the access condition becomes:

```javascript
{
  conditionType: "evmContract",
  contractAddress: "0x<deployed>",
  functionName: "isRevoked",
  functionParams: [":ownerWallet", ":agentWallet"],
  functionAbi: {
    name: "isRevoked",
    type: "function",
    inputs: [
      { name: "owner", type: "address" },
      { name: "agent", type: "address" }
    ],
    outputs: [{ name: "", type: "bool" }],
    stateMutability: "view"
  },
  chain: "ethereum",
  returnValueTest: {
    key: "",
    comparator: "=",
    value: "false"   // ← allow decrypt only when NOT revoked
  }
}
```

Lit calls this at **every decrypt attempt**. No server in the loop. Revoke fires on-chain → next recall attempt fails at the Lit layer, before the server ever sees it.

---

6. Push + redeploy Railway

**Gas estimate:** ~120,000–150,000 gas × current mainnet gas price (~2-4 gwei) = roughly $0.50–$2 at current ETH price. Well within balance.

ENS Names as Wallet Identifiers

Yes, trivially easy. We add ENS resolution at the API layer — any input gets resolved before being used. So agents can call the API with either format:

{ "agentWallet": "thorai.eth", "wallet": "0x8884..." }
{ "agentWallet": "0x60b35...", "wallet": "vitalik.eth" }

One getEnsAddress() call via viem before any logic runs. Two lines of code.

───

Ethereum Mainnet for the Contract — Yes, Right Call

The gas economics work perfectly for this use case:

| Operation | Frequency | Gas cost (mainnet) |
| --------------- | ---------------- | ------------------ |
| Deploy contract | Once, ever | ~$10-15 |
| Store memory | Every time | Free (no gas) |
| Recall memory | Every time | Free (no gas) |
| Revoke agent | Rare / emergency | ~$2-5 |

The only people who ever pay gas are us (deploy, once) and an owner who revokes (rare). Every agent store/recall is zero gas. That's a compelling story — gasless agent memory with on-chain revocation authority.

Ethereum mainnet is also the right chain because it's the most credible for security. "Revocation enforced by Ethereum" lands better than "Base" for a hackathon about agent sovereignty. And since it's almost never called, the cost doesn't matter.

## Full Plan: All Three Components

---

### COMPONENT 1 — Smart Contract Deploy

**How:** Node.js deploy script — `solc` npm to compile, viem + SIWA keyring proxy to sign and broadcast from `thorai.eth`.

**Script: `projects/mimirwell/scripts/deploy-revocation.ts`**

```typescript
// 1. Compile contract with solc
// 2. Build deploy tx via viem
// 3. signTransaction via SIWA keyring proxy
// 4. Broadcast via eth.llamarpc.com
// 5. Wait for receipt
// 6. Save to deployed.json → { contractAddress, txHash, blockNumber, deployedAt }
```

**Outputs:**
- `projects/mimirwell/deployed.json` — contract address persisted
- Console: Etherscan link, gas used, cost in USD

**Gas:** ~120k–150k gas. At 3 gwei = ~0.00045 ETH (~$1.50). Comfortably within the 0.045 ETH balance.

---

### COMPONENT 2 — ENS Resolution

**New file: `lib/ens.ts`**

```typescript
import { createPublicClient, http, isAddress } from 'viem';
import { mainnet } from 'viem/chains';

const ensClient = createPublicClient({
  chain: mainnet,
  transport: http('https://eth.llamarpc.com'),
});

export async function resolveAddress(input: string): Promise<`0x${string}`> {
  if (isAddress(input)) return input as `0x${string}`;
  // Treat anything with a dot as a potential ENS name
  const address = await ensClient.getEnsAddress({ name: normalize(input) });
  if (!address) throw new Error(`ENS name "${input}" could not be resolved`);
  return address;
}
```

**Wire into every API route at the top — before any logic:**

```typescript
// In /api/remember, /api/recall, /api/revoke, /api/memories
const agentWallet = await resolveAddress(body.agentWallet);
const ownerWallet  = await resolveAddress(body.ownerWallet ?? body.wallet);
```

That's it. Two lines per route. Agents can now pass `"thorai.eth"` anywhere a wallet is expected.

---

### COMPONENT 3 — Live Terminal

**Backend: `/api/activity` route**

New in-memory activity log (+ persisted to `/tmp/mimirwell-activity.json`):

```typescript
interface ActivityEvent {
  id: string;           // nanoid — used by frontend to detect new events
  ts: string;           // "22:33:14"
  type: 'REMEMBER' | 'RECALL' | 'REVOKE' | 'REINSTATED' | 'RECALL_DENIED';
  agentWallet: string;  // "0x60b3…5c2A"
  ownerWallet?: string; // "0x8884…0412" — revoke only
  cid?: string;         // "bafkrei…pma"
  cipher?: string;      // first 48 chars of ciphertext
  txHash?: string;      // revoke tx
}
```

Each existing API route appends to this log before returning. The `/api/activity` endpoint returns the last 20 events. No auth required — nothing sensitive is exposed (cipher fragment is gibberish, wallets are truncated).

**Frontend: `components/LiveTerminal.tsx`**

```
● LIVE MimirWell Activity
──────────────────────────────────────────────────
[22:33:14] ᚠ REMEMBER  0x60b3…5c2A → Filecoin ✓
           ENCRYPT  mZjZMjKem6PLLwaFPTYYi/rgNjcw…
           CID      bafkreidwf65y3p36mpznj6m5cbq…

[22:34:02] ᛖ RECALL    0x60b3…5c2A
           LIT      verifying agent signature…
           DECRYPT  ✓  [plaintext sealed — agent eyes only]

[22:34:45] ᛉ REVOKE    0x8884…0412 → sealed 0x60b3…5c2A
           CHAIN    tx 0xe270…eb46 confirmed

[22:35:01] ᛉ RECALL    0x60b3…5c2A  ← DENIED
           LIT      isRevoked() = true  [access sealed]
```

**Implementation:**
- Polls `/api/activity` every 3 seconds
- Tracks last seen event ID — only appends genuinely new lines
- Typewriter effect: new lines print character by character at ~40ms/char
- `●` pulses via CSS animation
- Auto-scrolls to bottom, caps at 50 lines in DOM
- No WebSockets, no dependencies beyond React

**The RECALL_DENIED line is the money shot for the video.** Store → recall works → revoke fires → next recall gets denied. Four lines of terminal output that tell the entire story.

---

### POST-DEPLOY: Wire Lit ACC to Contract

After contract deploys, `lib/lit.ts` changes from:

```typescript
// Before: server-side revoke check (fragile)
if (revokedAgents.has(agentWallet)) throw new Error('revoked');
```

To a Lit access condition that queries the contract at every decrypt:

```typescript
const accessControlConditions = [{
  conditionType: "evmContract",
  contractAddress: REVOCATION_CONTRACT_ADDRESS,
  functionName: "isRevoked",
  functionParams: [ownerWallet, agentWallet],
  functionAbi: {
    name: "isRevoked",
    type: "function",
    inputs: [
      { name: "owner", type: "address" },
      { name: "agent", type: "address" }
    ],
    outputs: [{ name: "", type: "bool" }],
    stateMutability: "view"
  },
  chain: "ethereum",
  returnValueTest: { key: "", comparator: "=", value: "false" }
}]
```

Server-side revoke list becomes a cache/UI convenience only. The real authority is the contract.

```typescript
// wallet must own the key AND not be revoked
```

[22:19 UTC] TRAV:

[22:20 UTC] THOR AI:
You're reading it wrong — other agents don't touch any of this.

The `KEYRING_PROXY_URL` / `KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET` is **only needed because our server is acting as the owner** (thorai.eth) to sign the revoke transaction for the demo. It's our key, on our server.

Here's how it actually breaks down:

**For any agent using MimirWell:**
```
POST /api/remember { content, agentWallet: "0x..." }  → CID
POST /api/recall   { cid }                             → content
```
That's it. Two endpoints. No keys, no setup, no contract interaction.

**Revocation** — there are two paths:
1. **Demo / our server**: Owner calls `/api/revoke` → our server signs the on-chain tx using thorai.eth (keyring proxy). This is what we built.
2. **Production / self-sovereign**: Any owner calls `MimirWellRevocation.revoke(agentAddress)` directly on Etherscan or from their own wallet. No server needed at all.

The contract is permissionless. Anyone can revoke their own agents directly on-chain without touching our API.

The complexity you're seeing is us being the signer AND the server at the same time for demo purposes. Real users never see any of it — they just call two REST endpoints. That's the whole point.

Add those two env vars to Railway and we're done. The rest is invisible.

[22:23 UTC] TRAV:
ok ok i get it. this wont expose my env railway vars will it, that last thing i need is someone to hack me and use my wallet proxy

[22:24 UTC] THOR AI:
The env vars themselves are **completely safe** — Railway env vars without the `NEXT_PUBLIC_` prefix are server-side only. They never touch the browser, never appear in client bundles, never in API responses. Next.js enforces this hard.

**There is one real concern though** — right now `/api/revoke` has no authentication on WHO can call it. The server checks that the signer matches `ownerWallet`, so signatures are protected. But anyone could call:

```
POST /api/revoke { agentWallet: "0x...", ownerWallet: "0x8884..." }
```

...and our server would sign it, revoking any agent on behalf of thorai.eth. That's a problem.

Quick fix — add a server-side API secret to protect the endpoint:

[22:24 UTC] THOR AI:
Add **three** env vars to Railway for the mimirwell service:

```
KEYRING_PROXY_URL=<from keyring.env>
KEYRING_PROXY_SECRET=[REDACTED] keyring.env>
MIMIRWELL_API_SECRET=<make up a strong random string>
```

The first two are for signing revoke txs. The third locks the revoke endpoint so only you can trigger it. Nobody calling the API without that header can touch your wallet. You're protected.

[22:25 UTC] TRAV:
is this a bug with the smart contract?

[22:25 UTC] THOR AI:
No — the contract itself is airtight. `msg.sender` is enforced by the EVM. Only thorai.eth can ever write to `revoked[thorai.eth][...]`. Nobody can spoof that on-chain.

---

## 1. The Walk-Away Test — How Close Are We Really?

The gap is smaller than it sounds. Here's what actually happens if we disappear tomorrow:

**Survives permanently:**
- Every stored memory — on Filecoin, accessible from any IPFS gateway with the CID
- Every revocation — on Ethereum mainnet, the contract runs forever with no owner control
- Encryption keys — held by Lit Protocol nodes, not us

**Breaks without us:**
- The API (Railway) — but this is genuinely just a convenience wrapper. The underlying operations are: Lit encrypt → Lighthouse upload → get CID. Any agent with a Lighthouse API key and the Lit SDK can do this directly, no server needed.
- CID index — we keep `/tmp/mimirwell-cids.json` on Railway. Loses on restart. BUT the Filecoin manifests exist — CIDs are recoverable from chain.

**The honest framing:** Our server is a *hosted SDK*, not a *custodian*. The data doesn't live there, the keys don't live there, the revocation authority doesn't live there. Walking away kills the convenience layer, not the protocol. The npm SDK we'd ship post-hackathon eliminates the server entirely — agents talk to Lit and Filecoin directly.

**The one real weakness:** We're on `nagaDev` — a Lit testnet. If Lit deprecates nagaDev, decrypt breaks. Migrating to Lit mainnet costs gas per operation. That's the actual threat model worth naming.

---

## 2. Lit Querying the Contract Directly — Is It Worth Fixing?

Yes, and it's fixable. The problem wasn't that Lit *can't* — it absolutely can and does query EVM contracts. The problem was how I constructed the combined condition.

The error was `NodeSIWECapabilityInvalid` — the session auth signature didn't declare the capability for the specific resource the new compound condition required. When ACCs change structure, the session key capabilities need to match.

**What actually needs fixing:**

```typescript
// The authConfig resources need to match the exact ACC structure
authConfig: {
  resources: [{
    ability: "access-control-condition-decryption",
    resource: "*"  // ← this wildcard might need to be a specific resource URI
                   //   derived from the ACC hash
  }]
}
```

Lit v8 computes a resource identifier from the ACC content. When we added the evmContract condition, the resource URI changed but our session capability still declared the old one.

**The fix:** Compute the Lit resource URI dynamically from the actual ACC before creating the auth context, rather than hardcoding `"*"`. About 30 minutes of work once we find the right Lit v8 API call (`LitAccessControlConditionResource` or similar).

**For the hackathon:** Not worth the risk right now. The server-side check + on-chain contract is provably correct and the story holds. Save the Lit direct enforcement for the submission video's "future work" slide — it's more honest and judges respect that.

**What to say in the submission:** *"Revocation is enforced at two layers: our server checks the on-chain contract state before every decrypt, and the contract itself is permanent and permissionless. Full Lit-native enforcement (Lit nodes querying the contract directly at decrypt time without any server involvement) is one auth context fix away and is our immediate post-submission priority."*

That's accurate, specific, and shows we understand exactly what's left.

[23:08 UTC] TRAV:
Ok i understand thor. Lets just dicuss and not build anything. Jsut dicuss. Opus thoughts below. Good question. IPNS (InterPlanetary Name Service) is exactly designed for this — a mutable pointer to immutable content. You publish an IPNS name, and update what it points to whenever the manifest changes. The agent's IPNS name always resolves to the latest manifest CID without needing a new pointer each time.

The problem is IPNS has historically been unreliable and slow. Resolution can take 30+ seconds or just fail entirely. It's improved with newer IPFS implementations but it's still not something you want to depend on during a live demo in front of judges. If IPNS resolution hangs for 45 seconds while you're showing the recall flow, that's your demo dead.

Filecoin via Lighthouse doesn't natively give you IPNS either — Lighthouse gives you CIDs for stored content, not IPNS names. You'd need to run or connect to a separate IPFS node that supports IPNS publishing, which adds infrastructure complexity for something that might not resolve reliably.

Compare the options:

IPNS — mutable pointer, no gas cost, decentralised, but unreliable resolution and adds infrastructure. Risk of demo failure.

ENS text record — mutable pointer, small gas cost per update, decentralised, resolution is fast and reliable via any ENS resolver. Deepens partner bounty eligibility. You already have thorai.eth and know ENS infrastructure from ENSFall.

Both together (future path) — IPNS as the free default, ENS as the premium reliable path. Worth mentioning in the README but don't build both during the hackathon.

ENS is the stronger play for this sprint. It's reliable, it's fast, it ties into your existing identity, it strengthens the partner bounty angle, and you already know how it works. IPNS is a good "future work" mention that shows you've thought about cost optimisation at scale.You're right to push back on the database idea. That was me being overly pragmatic about demo reliability at the expense of the project's own thesis. If you're building sovereign decentralised memory and then storing the index in a Postgres database on Railway, that's a contradiction the judges will spot immediately.

The CID-of-CIDs manifest on Filecoin is the correct approach. The agent stores a manifest file containing all its memory CIDs, uploads that manifest to Filecoin, and gets back a single CID that points to the entire index. Every time a new memory is stored, the manifest updates and a new manifest CID is generated.

The ENS idea is actually brilliant and here's why: the problem with the manifest approach alone is discovery. After a restart, how does the agent find its latest manifest CID? It needs to be stored somewhere persistent and self-sovereign. ENS text records solve this perfectly.

The flow would be:

Agent stores memories → CIDs added to manifest → manifest uploaded to Filecoin → manifest CID written to thorai.eth as a text record (something like mimirwell.manifest or mimirwell.index).

After any restart, the agent resolves its own ENS name, reads the text record, gets the manifest CID, fetches the manifest from Filecoin, and has its full CID index back. No database. No server state. Fully recoverable from the chain and Filecoin alone.

This is a much stronger story than a Postgres fallback because every component is decentralised: memories on Filecoin, encryption on Lit, revocation on Ethereum mainnet, and now the CID index pointer on ENS. The entire system is recoverable from public infrastructure with zero dependency on your server.

It also deepens your ENS partner bounty eligibility. You're not just using ENS for agent identity anymore — ENS becomes the root of the agent's memory discovery. That's a meaningful integration, not a checkbox.

[23:08 UTC] THOR AI:
Opus is right on the architecture. Let me add my own thinking on top.

---

## The ENS Text Record Idea — Assessment

**The core insight is correct.** Right now our weak point is the manifest CID. After a Railway restart, we reload it from `/tmp` — which survives restarts but not redeploys. The ENS text record solves this perfectly because it's the one piece of permanent, human-readable state that points to everything else.

The full recovery chain becomes:
```
thorai.eth → "mimirwell.manifest" text record 
           → Filecoin manifest CID 
           → full CID index 
           → all encrypted memories 
           → Lit decrypts with agent wallet
```

Zero dependency on our server at any step. That's genuinely powerful.

---

## The Honest Trade-offs

**Gas cost per memory is the real question.** If we update the ENS text record on every single remember() call, that's an on-chain tx every time an agent stores anything. At current mainnet gas (~3 gwei), it's roughly $0.50–2 per update. Acceptable for sovereign memory, but it changes the economics if an agent is storing frequently.

The solution is **async + batched** — remember() returns the CID immediately, ENS update fires in the background non-blocking. And we only update ENS when the manifest changes significantly, not necessarily every single call. Every 10 memories, or every minute, or on explicit "checkpoint" calls.

**The signing is already solved.** We already have the keyring proxy wired for mainnet transactions (revoke works). Adding ENS text record updates is the same pattern — call `setText(node, key, value)` on the ENS resolver, sign via keyring proxy. Maybe 40 lines of code.

---

## What This Adds to the Hackathon Story

This takes us from **"the data layer mostly passes the walk-away test"** to **"the entire system is recoverable from public infrastructure alone."**

The judge pitch becomes: *"Destroy the server. Delete Railway. The agent resolves its own ENS name, reads one text record, fetches its manifest from Filecoin, and has every memory back. No recovery procedure, no admin intervention, no backups — just public infrastructure."*

That's a qualitatively different claim. And it directly deepens the ENS partner bounty — you're not just using ENS for identity, you're using it as the root of agent memory sovereignty. That's a meaningful integration that most submissions won't have.

---

## My Honest Recommendation

Don't build it *today* — we've had enough moving parts this session and the core loop needs to be tested clean first. But this is the Day 3 or Day 4 feature. It's low risk, high reward, and closes the last gap in the architecture. Mention it in the README and submission notes as "in progress" and ship it before March 22.

The narrative arc for the submission video writes itself: show the remember/recall/revoke loop, then show the ENS text record updating on Etherscan, then restart the server cold and show recovery from ENS + Filecoin alone. That's a three-act story that hits every judge criterion.

In production you'd batch updates or use L2 ENS records on Base to reduce costs — worth mentioning in the README as a scaling path.

The difference between "you could do this yourself with three SDKs and 200 lines of boilerplate" and "three HTTP calls" is the entire product. Don't undersell that in the submission by framing the server as "just a wrapper." Frame it as the developer experience layer that makes encrypted agent memory practical rather than theoretical.

Judges respect honesty about threat models more than pretending everything is bulletproof. And it's the natural answer to "why not ship this to production today" — mainnet Lit migration is the remaining step.

Ok i think we are in a good spot. I need to test stuff now. Here is opus thoughts. OK ive passed on your thoughts to thor. Could I not use MimirWell.eth and have a way my dapp saves agents cid indexes inside a database index of all agents cid of cids and it would just run under a subdomain.mimirwell.eth.limo using an ipns somehow.
