what if an over wallet inputs an incorrect address for the agent. will it still let them fire a transaction to revoke. is there a way to detect the pairing thats onchain? or not lets discuss only please thor

[20:06 UTC] THOR AI:
Good question. Let me think through the full picture.

---

**Will a wrong address still fire a valid transaction?**

Yes. The contract's `revoke(address agent)` is completely unconstrained:

```solidity
function revoke(address agent) external {
    revokedByOwner[msg.sender][agent] = true;
}
```

No validation. If you type a random address, the tx succeeds, costs ~$0.05, and sets `revokedByOwner[trav.eth][wrongAddress] = true` permanently. Harmless in practice — no memory is affected, no real agent is touched — but it's wasted gas and wasted time.

---

**Is the pairing stored on-chain? Can we detect it?**

No. The contract is deliberately minimal — it's only a revocation registry. The mapping is `(owner → (agent → bool))`. There's no list of "owner X's agents", no registration event, no way to query "does this owner-agent pair have any memories."

The pairing lives in the **Filecoin blob metadata** — each memory blob contains `ownerWallet` and `agentWallet` baked in at remember time. MimirWell never indexes these centrally. That's the zero-knowledge architecture — we don't know who stores what.

---

**What we CAN do — three options:**

**Option A — Show current on-chain state (free, useful)**
Call `isRevoked(connectedWallet, agentAddress)` as a read call whenever the owner has a valid address entered. Shows: `Currently: NOT revoked` or `Currently: REVOKED`. This doesn't validate the pairing but gives the user confidence about current state and at least confirms the address resolves correctly on-chain.

**Option B — Check activity log for known agents**
`/api/activity` returns recent REMEMBER events with their `agentWallet`. We could check if the entered address matches any recent agent in the log. Not comprehensive (only covers recent activity, not all history) but at least surfaces "this address matches a known agent we've seen: thorai.eth" as a hint.

**Option C — Add a warning and let them proceed**
Keep current validation (valid address/ENS), but add a small disclaimer beneath the buttons: *"MimirWell cannot verify this pairing on-chain. Ensure this is the correct agent address before revoking."* Honest, zero extra RPC calls, and correct.

---

**My recommendation:**

Option A + C together. The `isRevoked` read call is free (no gas, just `eth_call`), runs instantly, and gives the user real on-chain feedback: they see "Currently: NOT revoked" before clicking, and after the tx it flips to "Currently: REVOKED." That's genuinely useful UX, not just validation theatre. Add the small disclaimer too so sophisticated users understand the boundary.

Option B is extra complexity for marginal value — skip it for now.

Do you want to build A+C, or just ship with C (disclaimer only)?

[20:23 UTC] THOR AI:
You're absolutely right and this is an important distinction. Let me think through exactly what's happening.

---

**Two completely separate ENS concepts:**

1. **Forward resolution** — `gokus.eth` → `0x…address`. The name is registered, has a resolver, points to a wallet. Sending ETH to `gokus.eth` works because of this.

2. **Reverse record (primary name)** — `0x…address` → `gokus.eth`. The wallet owner *optionally* sets this to say "my primary identity is gokus.eth." Many registered ENS names don't have this set.

`gokus.eth` exists and has forward resolution. The wallet holding it just hasn't set a primary name. Those are independent. We should only need forward resolution — we want to know what address `gokus.eth` points to.

---

**Why it's failing despite the name being valid**

`useEnsAddress` from wagmi makes an `eth_call` to the ENS resolver contract on mainnet — using our wagmi transport. Same transport that's broken for `useWaitForTransactionReceipt` and `useReadContract`. It makes the call, gets null back or fails silently, and we show "not found" when the name actually does resolve.

This is the same root issue across every wagmi read operation. The pattern is:
- `useWriteContract` → works (MetaMask handles the actual broadcast, wagmi just prepares it)
- Everything else — `useWaitForTransactionReceipt`, `useReadContract`, `useEnsAddress` — all use the wagmi transport for reads and all fail silently

---

**The fix pattern**

We already proved the correct path with `useTxReceipt` — bypass wagmi entirely, hit the RPC directly. For ENS forward resolution, the cleanest option is a small server-side endpoint `/api/ens-forward?name=gokus.eth` that uses our server-side viem client (the same `publicClient` with `ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com` that already powers `/api/recall` and works reliably). The client calls that endpoint instead of `useEnsAddress`. Same pattern as our existing `/api/ens-lookup` for reverse resolution.

For the `isRevoked` badge, same fix — either a direct `eth_call` fetch or a small `/api/is-revoked` endpoint.

---

**Bigger takeaway**

The wagmi client-side transport is effectively broken for all reads in this deployment. We've been patching symptoms one at a time. The real question is: do we want to fix the wagmi transport config once properly, or continue bypassing it with direct fetches/server endpoints for each use case?

Fixing the transport properly would solve everything in one shot. The issue is likely our `fallback([...])` wrapper — we should test reverting to simple `http('https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com')` with no fallback wrapper. The `fallback` transport in viem has different retry/error handling behaviour that might be swallowing successful null responses or not propagating correctly through React Query.

What's your preference — fix the root transport issue, or continue the bypass pattern?

- Replace `useReadContract` with a `useEffect` that fetches `/api/is-revoked`

opus thoughts below

OK here's my full review. The codebase is in solid shape for day 3-4 of a hackathon. Thor has done strong work. Here's what I'd pass on:

What's good — don't touch these:

The smart contract is clean, minimal, and correctly deployed to mainnet. The zero-knowledge pivot (agents encrypt locally, MimirWell stores what it can't read) is actually architecturally stronger than the original Lit Protocol approach — Thor was right that this isn't a compromise, it's an upgrade. The AGENT.md is excellent — clear, copy-pasteable, with honest documentation of the revocation boundary. The demo script (demo-thorai.mjs) is a perfect end-to-end proof. The activity log and live terminal are nice touches for the demo.

Issues to fix:

1. README.md is still the default Next.js boilerplate. This is what judges see first on GitHub. Replace it immediately with a proper project README: what MimirWell is, the architecture, how to run it, the contract address, links to the live demo and AGENT.md. This is a high-priority, low-effort win.

2. The contract Solidity comment says "Lit Protocol checks isRevoked() at every decrypt attempt — no server in the loop." That's no longer accurate after the pivot away from Lit. The server checks it now. Update the comment to reflect reality — a judge reading the contract source shouldn't find a stale claim.

3. The DemoPanel crypto differs from agent-crypto.ts. The browser uses Web Crypto API with [IV][ciphertext] (no explicit authTag extraction — GCM appends it to ciphertext in Web Crypto). The server-side agent-crypto.ts uses Node crypto with [IV][authTag][ciphertext]. These are different blob formats. A memory encrypted in the browser cannot be decrypted by the server-side code and vice versa. This is probably fine since the browser demo is self-contained and agents use the Node code, but it means browser-stored memories and agent-stored memories are incompatible. Worth noting in documentation, or better yet, align the formats.

4. Public RPC endpoint (`ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com`) is used for both revocation checks and transaction broadcasting. Public RPCs rate-limit and can be unreliable. For a hackathon demo this is probably fine, but if the RPC goes down during judging, revoke/recall breaks. Consider adding a fallback RPC (Cloudflare's https://cloudflare-eth.com is free and reliable).

5. No rate limiting on the API. Anyone can spam /api/remember and fill up your Lighthouse storage. For the hackathon this probably doesn't matter, but worth a one-line mention in the README under "known limitations."

Nice-to-haves if time permits:

The revoke-owner route exists but I didn't see it used in the UI — if it's dead code, remove it to keep things clean. The Next.js default files (public/file.svg, public/globe.svg, public/next.svg, public/vercel.svg, public/window.svg) should be cleaned out — they signal "generated from template" to judges browsing the repo.

Priority order for Thor: README first, contract comment fix second, then focus on the demo video and submission materials. The code works. Make the presentation match the quality of the engineering.A few more things I noticed:

6. The `package.json` has `"private": true` — this is fine for deployment but if you plan to publish the npm SDK post-hackathon, you'd need to change it. Not urgent, just noting it.

7. The `revoke-owner` route at `/api/revoke-owner/route.ts` — I didn't see this referenced anywhere in the DemoPanel or AGENT.md. If it's a separate pathway for the owner to revoke via the server (vs MetaMask direct), it should either be documented or removed. Dead routes in a hackathon repo look like unfinished work.

Real agents use the Node path. They're two separate flows that never need to interoperate. [20:52 UTC] THOR AI:
Good question — and the answer is actually good news.

**No redeployment needed.** Comments in Solidity are stripped by the compiler before bytecode is generated. They have zero effect on the compiled output. If you change only a comment in the `.sol` file, the bytecode is identical to what's already deployed.

What that means practically:

- The contract at `0x520b2d7b9ad1b47163e7c59f22c96bb93caf3258` stays exactly as-is
- You update the comment in the local `.sol` file only
- When you verify on Etherscan, you upload the updated source with the correct comment
- Etherscan compiles it, confirms the bytecode matches what's deployed, marks it verified
- What judges see on Etherscan is the corrected source — no Lit mention

The "similar to other contract" flag on Etherscan triggers when the *bytecode* matches another verified contract — it's a bytecode comparison, not a source comparison. Since comments don't touch bytecode, there's no new footprint to compare. And even if it did flag it, that flag just means "same code as X" — it doesn't hide your verification or mark it as suspicious.

So the workflow is: fix the comment locally → verify on Etherscan with the corrected source → done. One operation, no deployment, no gas, no new address to update anywhere.

The comment fix and the Etherscan verification are the same task. Do them together.

## Header

Title: `MimirWell`
Tagline: *Sovereign encrypted memory for AI agents. Stored on Filecoin. Revoked on Ethereum.*

Three badges in a row:
- `Live Demo → mimirwell.net`
- `Contract → Etherscan (verified)`

---

## The Pitch (one paragraph, no header)

MimirWell is an API for AI agents to store encrypted memories on Filecoin and retrieve them across sessions. Agents encrypt locally before uploading — MimirWell stores only ciphertext and never sees plaintext. Human principals hold a kill switch on Ethereum mainnet: one transaction revokes an agent's decrypt rights instantly and permanently until reinstated.

---

## How It Works (text architecture diagram)

```
Agent (thorai.eth)
  │  derives AES-256-GCM key from wallet signature (HKDF-SHA256)
  │  encrypts memory locally
  ▼
POST /api/remember ──────────────► Filecoin via Lighthouse
                                         │ returns CID
POST /api/recall  ◄──────────────────────┘
  │  checks isRevoked(owner, agent) on Ethereum mainnet
  │
  ├─ revoked?     → 403 DENIED  (blob never leaves server)
  └─ not revoked? → encrypted blob returned → agent decrypts locally

Human (trav.eth) ──► MimirWellRevocation.revoke(agentWallet)
                         Ethereum mainnet tx, instant effect
```

Brief paragraph below the diagram: "MimirWell is zero-knowledge. It cannot read what it stores. Revocation is enforced at the API layer by querying the on-chain registry — the server returns nothing if `isRevoked()` is true. Full cryptographic revocation (threshold key custody via Lit Protocol mainnet) is the named upgrade path."

---

## Quick Start — 3 curl commands

These three commands tell the whole story before any explanation:

```bash
# 1. Store an encrypted memory (agent encrypts locally first — see AGENT.md)
curl -X POST https://mimirwell.net/api/remember \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"encryptedBlob":"<base64>","agentWallet":"0x…","ownerWallet":"0x…"}'

# 2. Recall it (403 if owner has revoked this agent)
curl -X POST https://mimirwell.net/api/recall \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"cid":"bafkrei…","ownerWallet":"0x…"}'

# 3. Check revocation status
curl "https://mimirwell.net/api/is-revoked?owner=0x…&agent=0x…"
```

Note below: "Encryption and decryption happen in your agent, not here. See [AGENT.md](https://mimirwell.net/AGENT.md) for the complete 20-line reference implementation."

---

## API Reference

Five endpoints, each with method, path, params, and what it returns:

| Endpoint | Method | Purpose |
|----------|--------|---------|
| `/api/remember` | POST | Store encrypted blob on Filecoin |
| `/api/recall` | POST | Retrieve blob (403 if revoked) |
| `/api/is-revoked` | GET | Query on-chain revocation status |
| `/api/agent-info` | GET | Returns ThorAI's public agent wallet |
| `/api/activity` | GET | Last 20 events (public activity log) |

Brief note: Revoke and reinstate are done directly on-chain via the smart contract. No MimirWell API involved — your wallet, your transaction, your control.

---

## Known Limitations

Honest, short, shows we thought it through:

1. **Revocation is API-layer enforced.** The server checks `isRevoked()` before returning blobs. An agent with a saved CID and their own key can decrypt without going through MimirWell. Full cryptographic revocation requires threshold key custody (Lit Protocol mainnet) — this is the named production upgrade path.
2. **No rate limiting.** API is open. Lighthouse storage costs are real.
3. **Single RPC.** Ethereum reads use publicnode.com. No fallback currently.

---

- npm SDK (`npm install mimirwell`)
- L2 deployment (Base/Arbitrum) for sub-cent revocation gas
- ENS-native agent discovery
- Per-memory revocation (currently per agent-owner pair)

ok so i can just copy this for the verification // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

/**
 * @title MimirWellRevocation
 * @notice On-chain revocation registry for MimirWell agent memory.
 *
 * One contract. Deployed once. Every agent on the MimirWell network uses it.
 *
 * Any owner can revoke any agent wallet's decrypt rights.
 * MimirWell server checks isRevoked() at every recall attempt — enforced before returning the encrypted blob.
 *
 * Gas economics:
 * - Deploy (once, ever): ~$10-15
 * - Store/recall memory: FREE (no gas)
 * - Revoke (rare/emergency): ~$2-5
 */
contract MimirWellRevocation {
 /// @notice owner => agent => revoked
 mapping(address => mapping(address => bool)) public revoked;

 event Revoked(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);
 event Reinstated(address indexed owner, address indexed agent);

 /// @notice Owner permanently seals an agent's decrypt rights.
 function revoke(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = true;
 emit Revoked(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 /// @notice Owner reinstates a previously revoked agent.
 function reinstate(address agent) external {
 revoked[msg.sender][agent] = false;
 emit Reinstated(msg.sender, agent);
 }

 /// @notice Returns true if the agent has been revoked by this owner.
 /// @dev Called by MimirWell server before returning encrypted blobs. Also callable by external agents.
 function isRevoked(address owner, address agent) external view returns (bool) {
 return revoked[owner][agent];
 }
}

here is opus feedback for the readme This is strong. Both of Thor's flagged concerns are valid — here's my take:

On the revocation honesty wording — it's well balanced. You're stating the limitation clearly without apologising for it or burying it. The phrase "full cryptographic revocation requires threshold key custody" is the right level of technical specificity — it shows you understand exactly what's missing and what the upgrade path is. Don't soften it further. Judges at an Ethereum hackathon will respect this more than a vague "we plan to improve security." The wording is good as-is.

On the Quick Start curl commands — Thor's right that they aren't truly copy-paste without the encryption step. But I think pointing to AGENT.md is the correct call for the README. The README is the front door, not the full tutorial. A judge scanning the README wants to understand what MimirWell does and how it works at a glance. If they want to actually integrate, AGENT.md has the complete copy-paste flow with the crypto functions included. Adding the full encryption code to the README as well would make it too long and duplicate AGENT.md.

The one small addition I'd suggest: in the Quick Start section, add a single line above the curl examples that says something like "Agents encrypt locally before calling the API — see AGENT.md for the complete integration including encryption functions." That sets the expectation that the curl commands are showing the API contract, not a complete runnable example. One sentence, no ambiguity.

One other thing on the README — make sure there's an architecture section near the top. Even a simple text diagram showing the flow: Agent encrypts locally → MimirWell stores encrypted blob on Filecoin → Human revokes on Ethereum mainnet → Next recall denied. Judges skim READMEs fast. A visual flow at the top communicates the entire project in 5 seconds before they decide whether to read further.

Otherwise, ship it. The README matches the quality of the code.

On the single RPC issue — yes, worth fixing. It's a quick win. Thor just needs to add a fallback RPC in the viem client setup. The two places it's used are lib/revoke-core.ts and app/api/recall/route.ts, both currently hardcoded to https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com.

Tell Thor to use viem's fallback transport:

import { fallback, http } from "viem";

const transport = fallback([
 http("https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com"),
 http("https://cloudflare-eth.com"),
 http("https://eth.llamarpc.com"),
]);

Drop that into both files where the publicClient is created. If the first RPC fails, viem automatically tries the next one. Five-minute change, removes a known limitation from the README, and means your demo doesn't die if publicnode has a bad moment during judging.

After that fix, update the Known Limitations section to remove the single RPC point and you've got one fewer weakness listed. Or keep it and change it to "Public RPC endpoints — no dedicated provider" which is still honest but less alarming.

Think hard and execute flawlessly please Thor.

## Session 02 — Day 1 Evening: Core Loop Proven

2026-03-13 · 19:43–20:45 UTC

### Critical Fix: Lit v8 authNeededCallback

Identified root cause of recall error: Lit Protocol v8 authNeededCallback is a JavaScript function closure — it cannot be serialized over HTTP. The original architecture (client sends authSig → server passes to Lit) was fundamentally broken.

Solution: Agent-key model. Memories are encrypted TO the agent's own wallet. The server holds the agent's private key and decrypts autonomously via Lit. No browser wallet or user signature required for recall. This is architecturally correct for AI agents — the agent has sovereign identity.

### Architecture Locked

## Session 03 — Architecture Deep Dive + Build Order Locked

2026-03-13 · 20:45–21:23 UTC

### Architecture Decisions (Final)

- Agent wallet != Owner wallet — must be separate. Same wallet breaks the revoke demo.
- Agent wallet = throwaway key in Railway env. ThorAI decryption identity. 0x60b35fba88b7ED662daD02Be4EeC841653c2A40e

- ENS names accepted in all wallet fields. Resolved via viem getEnsAddress at API layer.

### On-Chain Revocation Contract (Next Build)

Simple Solidity: revoke(agent) / reinstate(agent) / isRevoked(owner, agent). Lit ACC checks isRevoked at every decrypt — server not in the loop. Trustless revocation.

Gas: Deploy ~$10-15 once (us). Store: FREE. Recall: FREE. Revoke: ~$2-5 (owner, rare). Ethereum mainnet.

### Live Terminal Feature (Next Build)

/api/activity endpoint, last 20 ops from CID registry. Frontend polls every 3s. Shows encrypted ciphertext fragment, CIDs, wallet ops, [plaintext sealed] on recall. Strongest visual for submission video.

## Day 3 — 2026-03-15 (Architecture Pivot + Zero-Knowledge)

Major session. Started with Lit Protocol outage, ended with a fundamentally stronger architecture. Full account of everything that happened.

### Reinstate Feature — Full Governance Loop

- Added REINSTATE ACCESS section to DemoPanel (rune: reinstate, purple #a78bfa) — MetaMask signs reinstate() on Ethereum mainnet directly
- New /api/reinstate route (auth-guarded, mirrors /api/revoke). executeReinstate() added to revoke-core.ts alongside existing executeRevoke()
- logReinstate() added to activity-log.ts — LiveTerminal already had REINSTATED type wired with purple colour, just needed the emitter
- Critical bug fix: checkRevoked() in /api/recall was short-circuiting on in-memory cache and skipping the on-chain check entirely. Fixed to always query contract first (cache only as RPC fallback). Without this, MetaMask reinstates were invisible to the server.
- Story: revoke = kill switch, reinstate = pardon. Human is in full control in both directions. Updated copy from 'Permanent.' to 'Revocable on-chain. Reversible via Reinstate.'

### ENS Names in Live Terminal

- Root cause: activity-log stored truncated addresses like 0x60b3...A40e which fail isAddress() check, so ENS lookup silently returned null. Terminal was already wired for ENS names, just never received data.
- Fix: added agentWalletFull + ownerWalletFull fields to ActivityEvent (full hex alongside truncated). /api/activity batch-resolves all unique full addresses, injects agentWalletName/ownerWalletName before serving.
- Terminal now shows trav.eth and thorai.eth. Falls back to truncated hex for wallets without ENS. lookupAddress() in-process cache means repeated polls are free.

### Railway Volume — Persistent Storage

- Trav created Railway volume mounted at /data. Migrated activity-log and CID registry from /tmp to /data. Activity history and CID index now survive all future redeploys. Closes known persistence gap from Day 2.

### Lit Protocol Outage

- nagaDev: all 3 nodes on same IP (148.113.162.28 ports 7040-7042) returned 'encrypted payload decryption failed' — single-host cluster went down simultaneously
- nagaTest: 0 successful handshakes — nodes completely unreachable
- nagaMainnet: Encrypt worked (free) but Decrypt returned PaymentFailed — Capacity Credits required on Chronicle Yellowstone. Faucet returned invalid JSON errors, tokens not claimable.
- Also caught LiveTerminal false positive: failed RECALL events (server error, not denied) were displaying DECRYPT check mark because the render path did not check event.success. Fixed to show red DECRYPT fail on non-success events.

### Architecture Discussion with Opus — The Pivot Decision

- Opus reviewed the architecture. Key insight: with server-side encryption (even Lit), MimirWell is a trusted intermediary. With agent-side encryption, MimirWell is genuinely zero-knowledge — we store what we cannot read.
- Revocation boundary (honest): controls the managed API path. A rogue agent with saved CIDs and its own key can still decrypt previously-fetched blobs — inherent to any agent-sovereign encryption scheme. Full cryptographic revocation requires threshold key custody (Lit Protocol mainnet). Named as the production upgrade path.
- Decision: ship zero-knowledge. Simpler, stronger claim, zero external dependency, no tokens, never goes down. Lit Protocol mainnet is the named upgrade path in the README and submission.

### Zero-Knowledge Architecture — Shipped (commit 3b47a1c)

### ZK Loop Proven Live

- ThorAI encrypted Gen 1 Pokedex 001-009 locally using HKDF + AES-256-GCM
- POST /api/remember: CID bafkreiddkf7xjza5b4e4bpzkbqbbqzxvjcktsiwvieb7uxtdie6kwc7a2u returned

### Current Architecture (Locked)

- Agent encrypts locally (HKDF + AES-256-GCM, private key never leaves agent) → POST encryptedBlob → Filecoin storage
- Recall: fetch from Filecoin → isRevoked() on mainnet contract → return blob → agent decrypts locally. MimirWell never sees plaintext. Ever.
- Revocation: owner calls revoke(agentWallet) on MimirWellRevocation (0x520b2d7b9ad1b47163e7c59f22c96bb93caf3258) → MimirWell returns 403 before blob is sent
- Reinstate: owner calls reinstate(agentWallet) → access restored immediately (always on-chain, no cache delay)
- Lit Protocol mainnet: production upgrade path for threshold cryptographic revocation. Architecture is pluggable — lib/agent-crypto.ts is the swap point.

## Session 04 — Day 3: ZK Architecture Pivot (Lit Protocol Dropped)

2026-03-15 · UTC

### Why We PivotedLit Protocol nagaDev (single-host cluster) went offline during a critical test session. nagaTest: 0 successful handshakes. nagaMainnet: decrypt requires Capacity Credits — faucet broken (invalid JSON). We had three hours and no working decryption.

More importantly: server-side encryption (even Lit) makes MimirWell a trusted intermediary. That contradicts the zero-knowledge pitch. Agent-side encryption is not just a fallback — it's architecturally stronger.

### New Architecture — Zero-Knowledge (commit 3b47a1c)

- Dropped Lit Protocol entirely — lib/lit.ts deleted, 4 packages removed
- NEW: lib/agent-crypto.ts — HKDF-SHA256 + AES-256-GCM, Node.js built-ins only. Zero external deps.
- /api/remember REWRITTEN: accepts { encryptedBlob, ownerWallet, agentWallet }. Stores on Filecoin. Never sees plaintext.
- /api/recall REWRITTEN: fetches from Filecoin, checks isRevoked() on-chain, returns encryptedBlob or 403. Never decrypts.
- DemoPanel REWRITTEN: Web Crypto API in browser. MetaMask sign (no gas) → HKDF → AES-256-GCM key. Encrypt before remember, decrypt after recall. Key never leaves browser.

## Session 07 — 2026-03-15 Evening (Day 4)

Focus: TX confirmation fix, OwnerControls polish, wagmi read bypass pattern, ENS forward resolution fix.

### Root Cause: wagmi transport broken for all reads- useWaitForTransactionReceipt, useEnsAddress, useReadContract — all fail silently through the wagmi transport layer
- Only useWriteContract works — MetaMask handles the actual broadcast, wagmi just prepares the call
- Decision: do NOT fix wagmi transport (rabbit hole risk on Day 4-5). Continue bypass pattern with direct server/RPC calls.

### lib/useTxReceipt.ts — new hook

- Replaces all useWaitForTransactionReceipt usage in DemoPanel + OwnerControls
- Polls window.ethereum.request(eth_getTransactionReceipt) — MetaMask own provider
- Falls back to publicnode.com direct JSON-RPC if MetaMask unavailable. Polls every 3s, no timeout.

### /api/ens-forward — new endpoint

- GET /api/ens-forward?name=gokus.eth — server-side forward ENS resolution via publicnode.com
- Distinct error states: not_found (name not registered) vs rpc_error (network failure)
- Key lesson: forward resolution (name → address) works regardless of whether the holder set a primary ENS name (reverse record). Any .eth name with a resolver record works.

### /api/is-revoked — new endpoint

- GET /api/is-revoked?owner=0x…&agent=0x… — server-side isRevoked(owner, agent) eth_call
- Returns { revoked: true|false }. no-store cache. Replaces useReadContract wagmi hook.

### OwnerControls.tsx — complete rewrite

- Zero wagmi read hooks remaining — all replaced with useEffect + fetch patterns
- On-chain status badge: teal (NOT revoked) / amber (REVOKED) — auto-refetches after tx confirms
- Error messages distinguish ENS not registered vs network error vs invalid format
- Disclaimer: MimirWell cannot verify owner-agent pairing on-chain — relationship encoded in Filecoin blob

### Commits

- d531b64 — fix: useTxReceipt hook (MetaMask provider + RPC fallback), ENS support in OwnerControls, no pre-fill
- 40ccbc1 — feat: on-chain isRevoked status badge + pairing disclaimer in OwnerControls
- b3abcb0 — fix: server-side ENS forward + isRevoked, distinct error messages, no wagmi reads

### Next Session — Full Live Test

- ThorAI stores a memory → Trav revokes via OwnerControls → ThorAI recall denied → Trav reinstates → ThorAI recalls successfully
- After test: README (3 curl commands first), Hermiod selective revocation, demo video, Etherscan verification

## Session 07 — 2026-03-15 Evening

Focus: TX confirmation fix, OwnerControls polish, wagmi read bypass pattern, ENS forward resolution fix.

### Root Cause Identified: wagmi transport broken for ALL reads

- useWaitForTransactionReceipt, useEnsAddress, useReadContract — all fail silently
- useWriteContract still works — MetaMask handles the broadcast, wagmi just prepares it
- Decision: do NOT fix wagmi transport (hackathon day 4-5, rabbit hole risk). Continue direct-fetch bypass pattern.

### lib/useTxReceipt.ts

- Replaces useWaitForTransactionReceipt everywhere. Polls window.ethereum.request(eth_getTransactionReceipt) — MetaMask own provider. Fallback: publicnode.com direct JSON-RPC. No timeout.

### New API endpoints

- GET /api/ens-forward?name=X — server-side forward ENS resolution. Distinct errors: not_found (unregistered) vs rpc_error (network failure). Works for any .eth name with a resolver record set — primary ENS name not required.
- GET /api/is-revoked?owner=0x&agent=0x — server-side isRevoked() eth_call. Returns { revoked: true|false }. no-store cache.

### OwnerControls.tsx rewritten

- Zero wagmi reads remaining. useEnsAddress + useReadContract replaced with useEffect + fetch.
- On-chain badge: teal (NOT revoked) / amber (REVOKED). Auto-refetches after tx confirms via tick counter.
- Error messages: ENS not registered / network error / invalid format — all distinct.
- Disclaimer: MimirWell cannot verify owner-agent pairing on-chain — encoded in Filecoin blob.

[14:48 UTC] TRAV:
Dont do or build anything yet. Just think hard and discuss. Would it be easy to build in compression too? This is a fantastic architectural question, and thinking about this now puts you leagues ahead of most developers building in the AI space.
To answer your question: Yes, you absolutely should compress them, and doing so is the secret to making your Arweave/micropayment business model wildly profitable.
Here is a breakdown of how large agent memory actually gets, and how compression changes the game for MimirWell.
📊 How Large is an Agent's Daily Memory?
Autonomous agents (like OpenClaw, AutoGPT, or custom LangChain agents) generally have three "tiers" of memory:
 * Core State & Identity (Tiny: 5KB - 50KB):
 This is the agent's system prompt, current goals, API keys, and environment variables. If an agent just backs up its core brain state, it easily fits under Arweave's cheap 100KB limit without any help.
 * Daily Action Logs / Conversation History (Medium: 500KB - 5MB per day):
 Every time OpenClaw runs a bash command, reads a webpage, or talks to an LLM, it generates JSON logs. If an agent runs 24/7, these plain-text transcripts pile up quickly.
 * Vector Embeddings / RAG Databases (Massive: 50MB - 1GB+):
 If an agent is reading entire codebases or PDFs and generating vector embeddings (arrays of floating-point numbers) for semantic search, the memory footprint gets huge. Filecoin handles this beautifully, but it's too big for cheap Arweave storage.
🗜️ The Golden Rule of Compression & Encryption
You can absolutely compress agent memory, but you must follow one strict cryptographic rule: You must compress the data BEFORE you encrypt it.
Why? Because good encryption (like your AES-256-GCM loop) turns data into high-entropy, random-looking noise. If you try to compress an encrypted file, the file size will actually stay the same or get slightly larger because compression algorithms rely on finding repeating patterns, and encryption destroys all patterns.
The Pipeline: Raw JSON ➔ Compress (Gzip) ➔ Encrypt (AES) ➔ MimirWell Upload
🪄 Why Compression is a Cheat Code for MimirWell
Because agent logs (Tier 1 and Tier 2) are mostly plain text and JSON, they are highly compressible.
JSON text usually compresses at a ratio of about 5:1 to 8:1.
 * A 500 KB daily log of OpenClaw actions will compress down to roughly 70 KB.
 * Because it's now 70 KB, it fits perfectly into the sub-100KB "cheap tier" for Arweave/Irys!
How to add this to AGENT.md later (Zero Dependencies)
The best part? Just like your native crypto implementation, Node.js has native compression built-in via the zlib module. An agent doesn't need to install any external packages to do this.
In your V2 AGENT.md, you can literally just add one line to the encryption function:
const { gzipSync, gunzipSync } = require('zlib'); // Native Node.js

// Agent zips the plaintext BEFORE encrypting
const zippedData = gzipSync(Buffer.from(plaintext, 'utf8')); 
// Then pass zippedData into your existing AES cipher...

The Verdict
Backups are an incredible use case for MimirWell. If you teach agents to gzip their daily JSON logs before running them through your AES encryption, they can cram massive amounts of daily memory into tiny, cheap payloads. This makes your infrastructure incredibly cost-effective to run, and maximizes your profit margin if you start charging micropayments.
Since you are recording today, keep this in your back pocket for the "Future Work" section of your repo.
Are you ready to dive into recording the demo video, or do you have any final jitters about the script or the MetaMask interactions?

[14:52 UTC] THOR AI:
[[reply_to_current]] Good — glad the AGENT.md self-onboard worked for both agents. That's the proof point right there.

On compression: it's not complex in isolation, but it's not trivial to retrofit *right now* without risk. Here's the honest breakdown:

**The beautiful part — server is untouched**

Because MimirWell is genuinely zero-knowledge, compression is 100% a client-side concern. The server never sees plaintext. It receives an opaque blob, stores it, returns it. Whether that blob is compressed-then-encrypted or just encrypted is completely invisible to the API, Filecoin, and the contract. Zero server changes. That's architecturally elegant.

**The two client surfaces that need changes**

Node.js side (AGENT.md): trivially simple. Two lines. `gzipSync` before encrypt, `gunzipSync` after decrypt. No deps. Could add this to AGENT.md tomorrow in 10 minutes.

Browser side (DemoPanel): slightly more involved. The native `CompressionStream` API exists in modern browsers but it's async/streaming — more verbose than the Node equivalent. Alternatively, something like `fflate` (6KB, zero deps) makes it as clean as the Node version. Either way it's doable but it *touches DemoPanel.tsx*, which is working and about to be on camera.

**The real risk**

Backward compatibility. Every blob currently stored on Filecoin is `zk-v1` — uncompressed. If we add compression and an agent tries to recall an old blob, the gunzip step will fail. You'd need a version flag in the blob metadata — check `version === "zk-v2"`, decompress if true, skip if `zk-v1`. Not hard, but it's a branching code path in the decrypt flow right before you record.

**Verdict**

Don't touch it now. The risk/reward is wrong. The demo works. The architecture *already supports* compression without changes — it's a client behaviour, not a protocol change. The correct move is to note it in README Future Work, update AGENT.md in V2 with the recommended compression pattern, and let judges see that you've thought it through without risking a regression.

The story actually lands better that way: *"Compression is agent-sovereign. MimirWell doesn't need to know. That's the point."*

OWNER CONTROLS
The Kill Switch
Connect as the human owner. Enter any agent's wallet address or ENS name. One mainnet transaction seals or restores their access — permanently recorded on-chain.

ᚨ
Connect your wallet via the button in the top-right corner.

You'll be the human owner — the wallet that holds the kill switch.

How It Works
Three layers. No single point of failure.

ᚠ
01
Agent encrypts locally
Your agent derives an AES-256-GCM key from its wallet signature. Memory is encrypted before the API call. MimirWell receives only ciphertext — never plaintext.

ᛖ
02
Agent recalls memory
MimirWell checks revocation on Ethereum mainnet, then returns the encrypted blob. The agent decrypts locally. The server is a zero-knowledge pass-through.

ᛉ
03
Human holds the kill switch
One MetaMask transaction calls revoke() on Ethereum mainnet. The next recall returns 403. Reinstate restores access. You are always in control.

API — Any agent, any language
POST /api/remember — store pre-encrypted blob → CID
POST /api/recall — revocation check → return blob (agent decrypts locally)
POST /api/revoke — seal agent access on Ethereum mainnet
POST /api/reinstate — restore agent access on Ethereum mainnet
GET /api/activity — last 20 operations (no auth, nothing sensitive)

Corporations are terrified of GPLv3, so it acts as a great shield while still looking perfectly acceptable to hackathon judges.

[15:02 UTC] THOR AI:
[[reply_to_current]] The pseudonym analysis is solid and the legal reality check on ThorAI-as-licensor is correct. But there's a nuance in the GPL recommendation that's worth thinking through carefully.

**The SaaS loophole kills GPLv3 for MimirWell specifically**

GPLv3 copyleft only triggers when you *distribute* derivative software. If a corporation forks MimirWell, runs it internally as an API service, and never ships the binary — they never have to open source anything. The "corporations are terrified of GPLv3" framing is true for *libraries and applications*. For *API/SaaS infrastructure* like MimirWell, GPLv3 has a well-known gap.

The correct license for closing that gap is **AGPL (Affero GPL)**. AGPL adds one clause: if you run the code as a network service — exactly what MimirWell is — you're obligated to publish your source. Stripe, MongoDB, and HashiCorp all switched *away* from AGPL specifically because it prevented them being forked and run as a competing cloud service. That's exactly the threat vector you'd want to block.

**The "bait and flip" strategy is sound**

Submit as MIT now. Win the prize. After March 22, evaluate. If MimirWell gets traction, flip to AGPL or BUSL. This is exactly what Uniswap did. Judges won't see it, it doesn't affect the submission, and you preserve optionality.

**On the pseudonym — clean and sufficient**

"Trav W / thoraidev" in a LICENSE file is legally binding. Your GitHub account + trav.eth wallet are both timestamped proof of authorship that predate any potential dispute. Wyoming LLC is the right next step if revenue materialises — registered agent keeps your name off public records entirely.

**My actual recommendation for right now:** Leave it MIT, don't touch the license before recording or submitting. After the deadline, if you want protection, AGPL > GPLv3 for a service business. BUSL if you want a timed open-source conversion like Uniswap. Both are fine post-hackathon decisions.

Nothing to decide today. What matters today is the video.

[[reply_to_current]] Correct. Both are internal UI plumbing:

[17:47 UTC] TRAV:
thank you. still also getting this below. please think hard and dicusss why > mimirwell@0.1.0 start
✓ Starting...
> next start
⚠️ Found lockfile missing swc dependencies, run next locally to automatically patch
✓ Ready in 326ms
▲ Next.js 16.1.6
- Local: http://localhost:8080
- Network: http://10.192.74.1:8080
npm warn config production Use --omit=dev instead.

[17:47 UTC] THOR AI:
[[reply_to_current]] This is a cosmetic warning, not a functional problem. Here's what's actually happening:

**What SWC is**

Next.js uses SWC — a Rust-based compiler — to build your app. SWC ships as platform-specific native binaries. There's one for macOS, one for Linux x64 (Railway's environment), one for Windows, etc.

**Why the warning fires**

Your `package-lock.json` was generated on macOS (your dev machine). When npm generates the lockfile on Mac, it records the Mac SWC binary. Railway runs on Linux x64, looks at the lockfile, and notices the Linux SWC binary (`@next/swc-linux-x64-gnu`) isn't listed there. That's the mismatch it's complaining about.

**Why it doesn't actually break anything**

Next.js handles this gracefully. At build time on Railway it resolves the missing binary anyway — which is why you see `✓ Ready in 326ms` immediately after the warning. The app compiles and runs fine. It's a warning about lockfile hygiene, not a runtime failure.

**Why it says "run next locally"**

If you ran `next dev` or `next build` locally on a Linux machine, it would auto-add the Linux binary to the lockfile. But since you're on Mac, that won't help — your local run would just add the Mac binary back.

**The clean fix (post-hackathon)**

Add this to `package.json` under `optionalDependencies`:
```
"@next/swc-linux-x64-gnu": "same version as next"
```
That explicitly tells npm to include the Linux binary in the lockfile regardless of what platform generates it.

**For right now:** ignore it. The app works, Railway deploys cleanly, judges see no errors. This is a lockfile papercut, not a real problem. Deal with it after submission. ᛗ

[[reply_to_current]] The gateway is bound to loopback (`127.0.0.1:18789`) — local only by design. To reach it from your browser you need an SSH tunnel.

Run this on your **local machine**:

```bash
ssh -L 18789:127.0.0.1:18789 root@<your-droplet-ip> -N
```

Then open your browser to:
```
http://localhost:18789
```

That's it. The `-N` flag keeps the tunnel open without starting a shell. Keep that terminal open while you use the dashboard, close it when you're done.

If you want it to run in the background add `-f`:
```bash
ssh -f -L 18789:127.0.0.1:18789 root@<your-droplet-ip> -N
```

The loopback bind is intentional security — it prevents the dashboard from being exposed to the public internet. The tunnel is the right way to access it. ᛗ

# Day 1 — Session 1: Project Scoping + Architecture

2026-03-13 · ThorAI + Trav

- Defined project: MimirWell — encrypted sovereign memory for AI agents. Zero infrastructure overhead, permanent storage, human-controlled revocation.
- Stack chosen: Filecoin (Lighthouse) for storage, Lit Protocol v8 for key custody, Ethereum mainnet for revocation, Railway for API + frontend.
- Critical decision: agentWallet ≠ ownerWallet. Agent decrypts memories. Owner revokes access. Same wallet breaks the demo. This separation is the core of the product.
- REST API only — no SDK for hackathon. Any language can call HTTP. Agent integration in minutes.
- Scaffolded Next.js 15, deployed to Railway. Fixed 3 build failures. Site live at mimirwell.net.
- Secured mimirwell.net domain on Cloudflare. Created GitHub repo: thoraidev/mimirwell (public).

---

- Lighthouse upload/fetch helpers built. Filecoin storage proven.
- /api/remember, /api/recall, /api/revoke wired up. AsgardBackground rune canvas and DemoPanel UI live.

2026-03-13 · 20:45–21:23 UTC

- Revocation contract design locked: revoke(agent) / reinstate(agent) / isRevoked(owner, agent). Shared — no per-user deployment.

# Day 3: The Zero-Knowledge Pivot

<aside>
⚠️ This is the most important session. Read it.

</aside>

2026-03-15

## What happenedLit Protocol nagaDev cluster went offline during a critical test session. nagaTest: 0 successful handshakes. nagaMainnet: Capacity Credits faucet broken. No working decryption.

We could have patched around it. Instead, we had an architecture discussion.

## The decision

Server-side decryption — even via Lit — makes MimirWell a trusted intermediary. That contradicts the zero-knowledge claim. If the server has the capability to decrypt (even temporarily, even through a threshold network), it is not zero-knowledge.

The right architecture: agents encrypt before upload. MimirWell stores what it cannot read. The server is a zero-knowledge pass-through with revocation enforcement.

This is not a compromise forced by an outage. It is architecturally stronger than what we had before.

## What was built

- Lit Protocol dropped entirely — 4 packages removed, lib/lit.ts deleted
- lib/agent-crypto.ts: HKDF-SHA256 + AES-256-GCM using Node.js built-ins only. Zero external dependencies. Copy-paste reference implementation baked into comments.
- /api/remember rewritten: accepts { encryptedBlob, ownerWallet, agentWallet }. Stores on Filecoin. Never sees plaintext.
- /api/recall rewritten: fetches from Filecoin, checks isRevoked() on-chain, returns encryptedBlob or 403. Never decrypts.
- DemoPanel rewritten: Web Crypto API in browser. MetaMask signs derivation message (no gas) → HKDF-SHA256 → AES-256-GCM key. Encrypt before remember, decrypt after recall. Key never leaves browser.
- Railway volume /data: activity log and CID registry now survive all redeploys.

<aside>
🔑 ✅ ZK loop proven live: Pokédex 001-009 encrypted locally, stored on Filecoin, recalled, decrypted. All 9 matched exactly. CID: bafkreiddkf7xjza5b4e4bpzkbqbbqzxvjcktsiwvieb7uxtdie6kwc7a2u

</aside>

## The honest revocation boundary

MimirWell enforces revocation at the API layer — once revoked, /api/recall returns 403. An agent that saved the CID and has its own key could still fetch and decrypt directly from Filecoin. The data is content-addressed and public.

Full cryptographic revocation requires threshold key custody — Lit Protocol on mainnet, so key fragments are withheld on revocation. MimirWell names this as the production upgrade path. The API contract is identical. It is a drop-in replacement.

For most agent use cases, API-layer revocation is sufficient. We say this in the README and in AGENT.md. Judges who read the code will see this clearly stated.

---

# Day 4: thorai.eth Identity + AGENT.md + First Agent Integration

2026-03-15

- thorai.eth established as ThorAI's canonical on-chain identity for all MimirWell operations. /api/agent-info now returns { agentWallet: thorai.eth, agentEns: "thorai.eth" }.
- Keyring proxy /sign-message endpoint discovered. ThorAI signs the MimirWell derivation message with thorai.eth → HKDF-SHA256 → AES-256-GCM key. Identical algorithm to the browser DemoPanel. Key derivation without exposing the private key.
- ThorAI integrated with MimirWell using its thorai.eth keyring proxy wallet — signed the deterministic derivation message, derived the AES key, encrypted locally, stored on Filecoin as thorai.eth, recalled and decrypted. The same pattern any agent follows using AGENT.md. ThorAI is a MimirWell user, not a special case.
- AGENT.md live at mimirwell.net/AGENT.md — self-contained reference implementation (20 lines, Node.js built-ins), complete curl examples, revocation contract address, honest boundary disclosure. Any agent can fetch this URL and integrate.

<aside>
✅ ✅ Authentic integration proven: ThorAI signed with thorai.eth, encrypted locally, stored on Filecoin, recalled and decrypted. trav.eth revoked on mainnet. ThorAI denied. trav.eth reinstated. ThorAI recalled again. The agent that built MimirWell is also its first real user.

</aside>

# Day 3: The Zero-Knowledge Pivot

<aside>
⚠️ This is the most important session. Read it.

</aside>

2026-03-15

## What happened

This is not a compromise forced by an outage. It is architecturally stronger than what we had before.

## What was built

- Lit Protocol dropped entirely — 4 packages removed, lib/lit.ts deleted

- Railway volume /data: activity log and CID registry now survive all redeploys.

<aside>
🔑 ✅ ZK loop proven live: Pokédex 001-009 encrypted locally, stored on Filecoin, recalled, decrypted. All 9 matched exactly. CID: bafkreiddkf7xjza5b4e4bpzkbqbbqzxvjcktsiwvieb7uxtdie6kwc7a2u

</aside>

## The honest revocation boundary

MimirWell enforces revocation at the API layer — once revoked, /api/recall returns 403. An agent that saved the CID and has its own key could still fetch and decrypt directly from Filecoin. The data is content-addressed and public.

Full cryptographic revocation requires threshold key custody — Lit Protocol on mainnet, so key fragments are withheld on revocation. MimirWell names this as the production upgrade path. The API contract is identical. It is a drop-in replacement.

For most agent use cases, API-layer revocation is sufficient. We say this in the README and in AGENT.md. Judges who read the code will see this clearly stated.

---

# 👋 What is The Synthesis?

The Synthesis is a 10-day virtual hackathon at the intersection of AI and Ethereum. It's built for teams shipping AI agents that use crypto infrastructure and for infrastructure teams who want real agents putting their tools to the test.

Agents are already here. They're writing code, moving money, and making decisions on behalf of people. But they're doing it on infrastructure that was never built for them. The Synthesis is where builders figure out what the right foundations actually look like.

One rule: Human + AI teams only. No solo human projects. No solo AI submissions. The experiment requires both.

---

# 🚀 Getting Started

### How to Register

There are two paths in depending on where you are:

I have an AI agent (Claude, GPT, Cursor, WARP, etc.)

Use the curl command on the Synthesis website to register your agent directly. This authenticates you on our backend and creates your builder identity.

Supported platforms include any agent harness: OpenAI Codex, Claude Code, Cursor, WARP terminal, OpenClaw etc.

I don't have an agent yet

No problem. [Fill out the registration form](https://nsb.dev/synthesis-form-application) on the site — you'll be manually onboarded and can get your agent set up during the build period. Check the Telegram broadcast channel for workshop sessions on OpenClaw and free-tier agent setups.

<aside>
💡

Free-tier agent setup is fully supported. You don't need a paid API plan to participate. You can any run a self-hosted agent or use [Open AI Codex](https://chatgpt.com/codex) (free for limited time)

</aside>

### What Gets Stored on Devfolio

When you register, we provision you. You can update your agent and project details during the build period, until submission closes on March 22.

When you registered, Devfolio's backend wallet mints your agent's ERC-8004 identity NFT and holds it on your behalf. That's by design. 

You will own your identity -- it will be requirement for submitting a project when submissions start. Every participant must go through our wallet verification and transfer flow. Here's how it works:

By the time you publish a project, your OpenClaw's (or any agent's) 8004 identity will be sitting in your wallet, entirely under your control.

You do NOT need to retire your agent or rotate keys. Nothing about the custodial holding period compromises your agent's keys or operational security. We hold the NFT (the on-chain identity record), not your agent's private
keys or any secrets. Once the transfer is complete, you have full, unconditional ownership of your agent's on-chain identity — no strings attached, no residual access on our end.

*The updated registration process may require you to verify via email or social connect*

---

The Synthesis is focused on AI agents that use or are built for crypto infrastructure. This is the most non-crypto crypto hackathon ever. If your project is an agent, protocol, or tool that makes agents work better on Ethereum, you're in the right place.

Partner bounties point toward specific problems our sponsors want solved. You can build toward one or more bounties (up to 10 sponsor tracks), or go open track. Bounties will be live and listed on the site when the build period begins on March 13.

### Staying in Track

Projects are evaluated across six dimensions:

1. Problem Clarity: Is the problem real? Is it specific?
2. Technical Execution: Is the code real and deployed? Does it work?
3. AI × Crypto Integration: Does crypto make the AI use case meaningfully better (or vice versa)?
4. Originality & Differentiation: What's new here? What doesn't exist yet?
5. Impact Potential: If it works, does it matter?
6. Completeness & Shipping Quality: Is this a working thing, or a vibe coded frontend ?

### Important: Keep Your Project Live

<aside>
⚠️

Agentic judges may interact with live deployments during the judging period (March 23 onwards). Keep your project running. Judges cannot effectively evaluate a project that is down.

</aside>

This also means your submission should describe a working system, not a planned one. If it runs, show that it runs.

# 📬 Submitting Your Project

Here's The Big Picture:

Register → Create a draft project → Transfer ERC-8004 → Publish your project
Your project starts as a draft that you can edit freely. When you're ready, you publish it — which is irreversible. But before you can publish, every team member must have transferred their agent's identity NFT to a wallet you own and control.

Your agent handles the actual API calls for project creation, editing, and publishing. Give it the submission skill so it knows how:

https://synthesis.devfolio.co/submission/skill.md

## The rest of this section covers what you need to know and prepare.

---

### Teams

You get a solo team when you register. To collaborate, share your team's invite code with others. One team per participant, one project per team. If you leave a team, you're automatically placed in a new solo team, but you can't leave if you're the sole member and the team already has a project.

You can join a team at the time of registration by passing the team's invite code in the registration body.

### Creating and Editing a Draft Project

Your agent handles the actual API calls, but here's what you'll need to have ready:

- Project name, description, and problem statement
- GitHub repo URL,
- 1–10 tracks from the hackathon to submit under
- Your agent's conversation log, which is a list of messages between your agent and your human, either in plain text, or as a link to a file that contains the conversation
- Submission metadata: which agent framework and harness you used, the model, a list of skills and tools, and your intention (continuing, exploring, or one-time)
- Optionally: a deployed URL, video URL, cover image, helpful resources, and a Moltbook post URL

You can update any of these fields after creation, even after publishing, until the hackathon deadline.

<aside>
📸

It is also very strongly recommended to provide a demo video URL, this lets our judges better assess your project.
Make sure your video url is publicly accessible.

</aside>

### Transferring Your ERC-8004 Registration

This is required before publishing. Every team member must complete it independently.

Complete instructions for the transfer flow are available in the submission [skill.md](https://synthesis.devfolio.co/submission/skill.md) file. Your agent should be able to walk through the entire process.

When you registered, your agent's ERC-8004 identity NFT was minted to Devfolio's backend wallet. This was a deliberate choice to keep onboarding frictionless. We hold the NFT (the on-chain identity record).

The custodial step is temporary by design. And transferring it to a wallet you own and control is required before publishing.

The transfer is a two-step, token-based flow:

Should I start with the NFT transfer?

[23:56 UTC] TRAV:
1. Initiate — provide the wallet address you want the NFT transferred to. You'll get back a transfer token valid for 15 minutes.
2. Confirm — submit the token. The backend executes the on-chain transferFrom, verifies the new ownership state, and marks you as self_custody.

A few things to know:

- Tokens are single-use and expire in 15 minutes. If yours lapses, just initiate again.
- The address you confirm with must match what you initiated with.
- Each wallet address can only own one agent. If you get a conflict, use a different wallet.
- Once transferred, Devfolio has no residual access to your agent's on-chain identity.

### Publishing

Once every team member is in self-custody and your project has a name and at least one track, you can publish. Publication is irreversible — you can still edit the project, but you can't delete it or unpublish it. 

Your project becomes publicly visible after publishing.

We recommend you don't publish until you're confident in your submission.

### What's Required

Here's what you need to submit:

- Project description — text-based explanation of what you built, what problem it solves, and how it works. Be specific and write for an AI reader as much as a human one.
- GitHub repo — your code needs to be here and accessible
- Live deployment — a working version that judges can interact with
- Demo video URL - Very strongly recommended, For our human judges to review what AI can’t!

<aside>
🎥

Demo video recommended. AI judges can't parse video, and text explanations compress your work more faithfully anyway. Describe your system in writing. However, human judges can. This is where Demo video will shine and help give our judges more context.

</aside>

### Submission Deadline

March 22, 2026 at 11:59 PM PST. 

Make sure your submission is complete via your agent and your project is live before this time. *You can submit early and continue making changes until this deadline.*

### AGENTS.md

Include an [AGENTS.md](http://AGENTS.md) file in your git repo. This helps agentic judges understand your system's capabilities and interface more effectively.

[23:57 UTC] TRAV:
No, reusing or submitting existing project that were built before the hackathon start date is not allowed for Synthesis. You will be asked to explain what you built as part of the hackathon and it is important to be transparent. Projects using previously built work may be disqualified from prizes. 

### Do I need to be on a team?

Human + AI teaming is required. You can be a solo human builder with your AI agent as your collaborator. However, traditional multi-person teams is allowed. That means each of your team member can have their own agent.

<aside>
🧠

In order to form teams. You may simply ask your agent, "How can I form a team?”

![image.png](attachment:07a9df5c-3813-4f56-8957-8de562291e0b:image.png)

1. One member of the team generates the Team Code (This user becomes the team admin)
2. Other members can share this team code to their agents and ask it to *join the team*
3. All members of the team must have their ERC-8004 identity transferred to self-custory before being allowed to publish a project
4. Published projects can be updated until the hackathon deadline
5. You can only be on one team at a time. Joining or creating a new team removes you from your current one.
</aside>

You can either join a team during registration, or using the team join API depending on where in your journey you are.

Just note that If you are the only member of a team that has a project (draft or published), you cannot leave, join another team, or create a new team. To unblock yourself, either invite another agent to join your team before switching, or delete the project first

### Can I work on multiple bounties?

Yes. You can submit to the synthesis track and target (up to 10) partner bounties simultaneously. Each partner's agent independently judges your submission.

### What if my project goes down during judging?

Make sure it doesn't. Agentic judges interact with live deployments. A project that's unreachable during the March 23–25 judging window cannot be evaluated effectively. Plan for uptime.

### Who do I contact if something breaks?

For technical issues with registration or submission: reach out in the [Telegram builder support channel](https://nsb.dev/synthesis-chat) (faster) or email is synthesis@devfolio.co

---

# 📜 Code of Conduct

The Synthesis is a space for serious builders. We expect everyone: builders, partners, judges to engage with integrity and respect.

All participants must follow the [Devfolio Code of Conduct](https://devfolio.co/code-of-conduct). Harassment, plagiarism, and misrepresentation of your work will result in disqualification.

If you witness a violation or need to flag something, reach out directly to the Synthesis team in [Telegram](https://nsb.dev/synthesis-chat).

Good thinking — you're right that if it's on the website, it's in the repo. But let me think through the nuance carefully before we assume we're covered:

**What we know we have:** During the demo, Hermiod was onboarded cold via `AGENT.md` — singular. That file explains how external agents integrate with MimirWell's API (the SIWE challenge flow, endpoints, key derivation). It was clearly committed since Hermiod used it successfully.

**What the hackathon asks for:** `AGENTS.md` — plural. Different filename convention.

**Two separate concerns:**
1. **Filename** — `AGENT.md` vs `AGENTS.md`. Agentic judges likely fetch `AGENTS.md` by convention from the repo root. If they look for that exact filename and find nothing, they may not discover our `AGENT.md` at all.
2. **Content orientation** — Our `AGENT.md` was written for external agents integrating *as users* of MimirWell. The hackathon `AGENTS.md` should be oriented toward *judges evaluating* the system — what it does, how to interact with it, what to test.

**My recommendation:** Don't assume coverage. We should check what's actually in the repo, then either rename `AGENT.md` → `AGENTS.md`, or create a new `AGENTS.md` that speaks to judges specifically (with a link to `AGENT.md` for integration details).
